Providers self-register at import time via `register_provider()`, and `sova/awareness/__init__.py:_auto_register()` imports each built-in provider module inside a per-module try/except `ImportError` so a missing optional dependency (e.g. `google-api-python-client` for `gmail`/`gcal`) silently drops that one provider from the registry instead of breaking the package import; `create_providers(config)` then instantiates only the providers named in `config.providers` that are actually registered, logging and skipping (never raising) both an unknown name and a provider whose `__init__` throws. `BriefingService.generate_briefing()` is the aggregation engine: it fans out `fetch_items()` to every configured provider concurrently via `asyncio.gather(..., return_exceptions=True)` so one provider's failure surfaces as a `ProviderStatus(ok=False)` entry rather than failing the whole briefing, then merges, categorizes, and sorts all items into a `Briefing` (attention items by descending urgency then recency, schedule items chronologically, informational items by recency, with schedule items excluded from informational so nothing is double-listed). `AwarenessConfig` (`sova/config/models.py`) is triple-registered like every other config section: the Pydantic model, `"awareness"` in `_NESTED_SECTIONS` (`sova/config/loader.py`), and settings metadata (`sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`); see "New config sections need quadruple registration" below for the fourth (env override) leg, which `AwarenessConfig` does not need since its `SettingsConfigDict(env_prefix="SOVA_AWARENESS_")` already covers flat fields and it declares no nested sub-model. Google OAuth2 (`sova/awareness/auth/google_oauth.py`) is shared by `gmail` and `gcal`: a single token at `~/.config/sova/google_token.pickle` (or `awareness.gmail_token_path`) covers both scopes, restricted to `~/.config/sova/` (`_validate_path()`) with `0o600`/`0o700` permissions, auto-refreshed on expiry and re-cached with a 60s in-process TTL to avoid re-reading the pickle on every provider call within a briefing. See `docs/awareness-setup.md` for the end-user GCP setup walkthrough and per-provider configuration reference. `sova doctor` reports one row per configured provider via `_check_awareness_providers()` (`sova/utils/diagnostics.py`), skipped entirely when `awareness.enabled` is false and running the `health_check()` calls concurrently with `return_exceptions=True`, the same shape as the dashboard's `get_provider_statuses()`. A name in `awareness.providers` that never reached the registry is reported as its own failed row, separately from a provider that instantiated but cannot reach its source. This is why `authenticate_google()` takes `interactive`: `GmailProvider.is_configured()` and `CalendarProvider.is_configured()` pass `interactive=False`, so a credential probe raises `FileNotFoundError` instead of falling through to `_run_oauth_flow()`, which opens a browser and blocks a local server until the user consents. Without that flag a diagnostic command (or a dashboard status poll) would hang indefinitely on an unauthorized machine; the interactive flow still runs from `fetch_items()`, which is what `sova briefing` calls. Files: `sova/awareness/base.py`, `sova/awareness/__init__.py`, `sova/awareness/briefing.py`, `sova/awareness/auth/google_oauth.py`, `sova/awareness/providers/`, `sova/utils/diagnostics.py`, `sova/config/models.py`.
- **Removing the config file means every registered setting needs a real editing surface, and two of them had none**: `sova.toml` was deleted from this repo in #900; `load_config()` still reads one if a project has it (auto-migrating into `.claude/sova.db` on first load), but SOVA never writes one and `sova install` seeds defaults straight into the database. Three gaps outlived that removal, all of them invisible because the config *model* was complete. (1) `sova.toml.default` survived as a 2026-07 snapshot nothing read: no code, no Makefile target, no installer copy, one stale mention in a spec table. It was actively misleading rather than merely dead, carrying `model = "opus"` (against the pin-explicit-model-IDs rule above), the deprecated `no_ai_coauthor` key, and roughly thirty missing sections, and issue #391 (filed six weeks before #900) sent a PR to add an `[awareness]` example to it. Deleted. (2) `renderSettingRow()` in `settings.html` gated the edit affordance on `isStructured = (value_type === 'list' || value_type === 'object')`, so all 20 list-typed settings were read-only badges, `awareness.providers` among them: the briefing page's own empty state links to "Configure providers in Settings", which could not configure them. The backend was already complete (`_cast_list()` parses a JSON array or comma-separated text, lists persist as JSON, `update_config()` validates), so the fix is the gate plus `_rawToInputString()`, shared by `editConfig()`'s input seeding and the blur no-change check so an untouched empty list is not read as an edit. Objects stay read-only: they have no single-line representation. (3) `sova config set` did not exist, despite five doc lines across `docs/awareness-setup.md` and `docs/jira-configuration-guide.md` instructing users to run it (one of them emitted at runtime as a log hint). `sova/cli/commands/config.py` adds it as a Typer sub-app whose `invoke_without_command=True` callback preserves the bare `sova config` table, routing writes through the same `settings_service.update_config()` the dashboard uses, so the two surfaces cannot validate differently. `"config"` joins `"doctor"` in `_CONFIG_TOLERANT_COMMANDS`: `sova config set` is the documented repair path for a config that no longer loads, so the startup guard must not abort it, and `_validate_config_consistency()` already fails open on an unloadable base config. The bare `sova config` path catches its own `load_config()` failure and points at `config set` rather than printing a traceback. `tests/test_settings_template.py` is the drift guard for (2): source-level, in the spirit of `test_model_literal_guard.py`, since the gate is JavaScript with no Python seam, and it also asserts no `SettingMeta.description` names `sova.toml` (five did, including three that read "Edit via [pipelines] in sova.toml", pointing at a file that no longer exists for settings the UI would not let you edit either). Files: `sova/cli/commands/config.py`, `sova/cli/app.py`, `sova/dashboard/templates/settings.html`, `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`, `tests/test_settings_template.py`, `tests/test_cli.py`, `tests/test_settings_service.py`. Issue #391. A self-review round found a fourth gap in the same PR: `sova config set` and the dashboard's `POST /settings/config` both write through `settings_service.update_config()`, but only the dashboard route follows with `_reload_all_configs()`/`sync_max_concurrent()` to push the change into a running process's in-memory state (the LLM provider singleton, `agent.runtime`, daemon components, agent-pool concurrency). A CLI invocation is a separate process from any `sova server`/dashboard that happens to be running, so it cannot call that dispatch even in principle for those specific settings, and previously gave no indication that anything was stale. `_warn_if_reload_required()` in `sova/cli/commands/config.py` prints a restart notice by reusing the router's own `_match_reload_target()`/`_RESTART_REQUIRED_PREFIXES` classification (a lazy import, not a duplicated copy), plus a `max_parallel_agents` special case mirroring the router's own special-cased dispatch.
- **Memory extraction is a no-op**: `ExtractMemoryStep` still runs before every handoff step in both pipelines (step slot preserved for future rule-based extraction), but `extract_memories()` returns immediately without calling the LLM. `ReviewerRole._extract_review_memories()` is also a no-op. Use the human-reviewed `/extract-knowledge` command for knowledge capture. Infrastructure (`_build_extraction_prompt`, `_parse_extraction_response`, `_deduplicate_and_store`) is retained in `sova/knowledge/extraction.py` for future use.
- **Issue Lifecycle Control**: `IssueLifecycle` is the "spine" connecting all `TaskRun` records for a single issue into a unified journey with 6 phases (`LifecyclePhase` enum: development, post_pr, review, address_review, integrate, post_merge). `LifecyclePhaseRecord` tracks each phase execution (status, cost, attempt counter, linked TaskRun). Phase transitions are advisory (warnings, not strict enforcement): matches `--force` philosophy. The `post_pr` phase is passive (no TaskRun; inferred from PR creation). `lifecycle_service.py` provides CRUD, phase transitions, and backward-compatible reconstruction from pre-existing TaskRuns via `build_lifecycle_view()`. Integration hooks in `agent_lifecycle.py` (`_link_run_to_lifecycle`, `_finalize_lifecycle_phase`) are non-fatal side effects. Dashboard UI at `/lifecycle/{issue_number}` shows a phase rail with live polling.
- **`classify_error()` is scoped to LLM invocation details and must not be pointed at arbitrary `TaskRun.error_message` text**: the reliability report (`sova/dashboard/services/reliability_service.py`, `/reliability` page, `/api/reliability/*`) is read-only aggregation over `TaskRun`, `StepExecution`, and `CostRecord`, with no new tables or collection. Its `classify_failure_cause()` runs an ordered, first-match-wins rule table (exact `step_hard_timeout`, the `fix_llm_timeout`/`fix_llm_failed` cycle prefixes from `sova/llm/errors.py:format_fix_llm_failure()`, the `Process exited with code N` shape from `_build_exit_failure_message()`, then free-text `GateCheckResult.reason` markers) before delegating what is left to `classify_error()`. That delegation cannot be unconditional. `classify_error()`'s contract is "name this failure's category", never "was there a failure", and two of its pattern sets match text any shell, git, or CI step produces: `_PROVIDER_UNAVAILABLE_PATTERNS` carries `no such file or directory` (whose own comment in `errors.py` says it is scoped to invocation-failure details, not arbitrary step errors) and `command not found`, and `_TIMEOUT_PATTERNS` carries the bare word `timeout`. Taken at face value, a failed `git worktree add`, a missing `shellcheck`, and a CI polling timeout all reported as an LLM outage in the one panel whose entire purpose is naming what to fix. The same problem applies to `_RATE_LIMIT_PATTERNS` (` 429`, `overloaded`) and `_BILLING_PATTERNS`/`_MODEL_UNAVAILABLE_PATTERNS`: this codebase has its own non-LLM rate-limit and quota subsystems (GitHub API rate limiting in `sova/supervisor/github_quota.py`, CI minutes budget in `ci_budget.py`, CodeRabbit review quota in `coderabbit_quota.py`) that produce "rate limit"/"429"/"quota"/"budget" text with no LLM vocabulary at all. All five ambiguous labels (`llm_provider_unavailable`, `llm_timeout`, `llm_rate_limit`, `llm_billing`, `llm_model_unavailable`) are therefore only accepted when the message independently names the LLM layer (`_LLM_CONTEXT_MARKERS`). That marker list deliberately excludes a bare `claude`: SOVA's own `.claude/` directory is in the path of nearly every git, worktree, and config error message, so it corroborates nothing, and only the quoted `'claude'` errno form plus `claude cli`/`claude code` are matched. It also excludes a bare `provider`: a non-LLM adapter error can name its own "provider" (e.g. a Jira `TaskAdapter` failure), which would satisfy the marker check and misclassify an unrelated adapter/network failure as an LLM outage, exactly what `_AMBIGUOUS_LLM_LABELS` exists to prevent. Anything unmatched falls into `other`, which is displayed rather than hidden, so a growing `other` bucket is the signal to extend the table. Two further properties: the per-cause "top failing step" lookup uses a correlated subquery carrying the same window predicate rather than a materialized run-id list (a busy window can exceed SQLite's bound-parameter ceiling, and blowing it would 500 the whole panel; mirrors `agent_status.py`'s `StepExecution.task_run_id.in_(select(...))`), ordered by id so a run with several failed steps reports its earliest deterministically; and `_FAILURE_STATUSES`/`_INCOMPLETE_STATUSES` must together partition `TASK_RUN_TERMINAL - {"done"}`, since `get_success_by_role()` promises `done + failures + incomplete == total_runs` and a new terminal status with no bucket would land in the total and in neither column. `TestStatusPartition` in `tests/test_reliability_service.py` pins that. Issue #979.
- **Doc counts drift after refactors**: step count, service count, adapter method count, and CLI subcommand list go stale after module splits, service extractions, or ABC changes. After any structural refactor, run actual counts (`find`, `grep -c`, `wc -l`) and update AGENTS.md, architecture.md, and CLAUDE.md. The `/review` command checks doc freshness automatically.
- **Test count in AGENTS.md is a rounded-down-to-the-nearest-hundred estimate, never an exact figure**: nearly every PR adds or removes a test, so an exact count (`"N at last count"`) went stale within one PR and, worse, produced a rebase conflict on the same line in every branch that happened to touch it concurrently, since two branches almost always disagree on the exact number even when neither changed AGENTS.md's surrounding content. The line reads `tests/ # pytest suite (8200+ tests)`, no second number. Update it only when the true count (`python3 -m pytest tests/ -q --collect-only | tail -1`) crosses into a new hundred; do not update it for single-digit or ten-scale drift. Issue #965/#777 rebase collisions.
- **Stale references persist after file/feature renames**: when a file is deleted, renamed, or a concept changes (e.g., "PAK" -> "SOVA"), references survive in commands, rules, issue bodies, and vision docs. After any rename or deletion, run `grep -rn "OLD_NAME" . --include="*.md"` to find stale references across all markdown files. Also check GitHub Issues and VISION.md for outdated naming.
- **Adapter-type guards must not assume GitHub**: service code that guards with `if not cfg.github_repo: return []` silently blocks Jira-backed projects. When checking whether a task source is configured, check the adapter type first: `if cfg.task_source.type == "github" and not cfg.github_repo`. Or remove the guard and let `create_adapter(cfg)` raise on misconfiguration (it validates required fields per adapter type). File: `sova/dashboard/services/queue_service.py`.
- **CI failure auto-recovery in MonitorCIStep**: when CI checks fail, `MonitorCIStep` enters an internal fix loop (modeled after `ValidateStep`'s pre-push hook fix pattern). The loop: (1) fetches CI failure logs via `get_ci_failure_logs()` (parses run ID from `CICheck.details_url`, calls `gh run view --log-failed`), (2) invokes Claude with the failure context to fix and commit, (3) runs local pre-push hook to validate before pushing, (4) pushes with `--force-with-lease`, (5) re-polls CI. Loops up to `min(ci.max_fix_attempts, 3)` times. Set `ci.max_fix_attempts = 0` in `sova.toml` to disable (pre-existing behaviour). Budget is checked before each attempt. The fix loop is entirely within the step's `execute()`: no pipeline structure, WorkflowEngine, or step registry changes. File: `sova/core/steps/monitor_ci.py`.
- **A CI check lookup must bind to the SHA it polled for, not the PR**: `MonitorCIStep._poll_ci()` previously looked up checks by PR number (`get_ci_checks()`, backed by `gh pr checks`), so a poll right after a push could see a prior commit's stale, possibly-green checks. `get_ci_checks_for_sha()` (`sova/git/pr.py`) queries GitHub's REST `commits/{sha}/check-runs` and `commits/{sha}/status` endpoints directly, so the lookup is bound to a specific commit. `_poll_ci()` resolves `expected_sha` from the local working tree's HEAD (`_get_local_head_sha()`) when not passed explicitly, and falls back to the PR-level lookup only when that resolution fails. `_check_ci_on_resume()` uses the same SHA-scoped lookup, but on `UnknownCommitError` it logs and returns `None` rather than treating the error as terminal itself; that falls through to `_poll_ci()`'s own poll loop, which is where the terminal (or retried) outcome is actually produced. `_check_existing_ci()` (the `ctx.no_new_commits` fast path) deliberately keeps the PR-level lookup, since no new push means there is no stale-SHA race to guard against. Binding to a SHA raises problems the PR-level lookup never had, each handled explicitly rather than left as a gap: (1) An empty SHA-scoped result is ambiguous between "no CI configured" and "GitHub hasn't registered checks for this commit yet", which is the state of every poll in the first seconds after a push. Past the `no_checks_grace_period`, `_pr_confirms_no_checks()` cross-checks the PR-level lookup, filtered through the same `ci.exclude_checks` patterns as the SHA-scoped list (otherwise a project whose only checks are excluded sees the unfiltered PR-level result as nonempty and polls to `max_wait` instead of passing), before converting the empty SHA-scoped result into a pass; a PR that does report (non-excluded) checks keeps polling (never a silent pass). This cross-check fails **closed**, not open: a PR-level fetch failure returns "not confirmed empty" rather than being read as "confirmed no CI", since the latter is the same silent pass the cross-check exists to prevent, just moved behind one failed API call; `max_wait` is the fail-closed terminal outcome in both the "PR reports checks" and "cross-check itself failed" cases. (2) A confirmed PR-head mismatch (another actor updated the PR after this pipeline's push, e.g. a human pushing a review fix, GitHub's "Update branch" button, or a concurrent SOVA run) rebinds `expected_sha` to the new head and keeps polling, rather than failing the step: that head move is a benign event unrelated to this pipeline's own work, and failing it would feed the supervisor's repeated-failure gate over someone else's push. The rebind also resets the no-checks grace clock to the moment of the rebind, not just the SHA: the overall `elapsed` clock can already exceed `no_checks_grace_period` by the time a mismatch is confirmed (the old SHA's checks took a while to complete), so without its own baseline the freshly bound head's first empty, not-yet-registered result would be immediately eligible for a false "No CI checks found" pass, which is exactly the registration-window race this design exists to close. `_find_pr_head_mismatch()` still fails open (treated as "no mismatch") on a fetch failure when checking the head, since a transient `gh`/network blip must not block a result forever. (3) A SHA GitHub has no record of at all (never pushed, or a push whose failure was swallowed upstream, e.g. `AddressExternalFindingsStep` logging a push failure at warning level and continuing) is permanently unresolvable, not a transient fetch failure: `get_ci_checks_for_sha()` raises `UnknownCommitError` only when **both** the check-runs and commit-status endpoints independently return a 404/422 "no such commit" response, since either endpoint alone succeeding proves the commit exists (a single endpoint's 404 is a Checks/Status API hiccup, not a failed push, and returns `None` like any other transient failure, which callers retry). Even a corroborated `UnknownCommitError` is not immediately terminal in `_poll_ci()`: it cross-checks `get_pr_head_sha()` first, and if the PR's own head still matches `expected_sha`, that is stronger evidence of a transient GitHub-side inconsistency than of a failed push, so it logs and keeps polling. An unreadable PR head (`get_pr_head_sha()` itself returning `""` on a transient fetch failure) is "no corroboration either way", not "confirmed bad push": it is treated the same as a confirmed match and keeps polling, mirroring `_find_pr_head_mismatch()`'s own fail-open contract; only a genuine, confirmed mismatch fails fast, naming the unresolvable SHA, instead of polling a result that can never change. (4) An unrecognized check-run `conclusion` on a completed run is logged and defaults to `FAILURE` rather than `None` (which would read as neither failed nor passed, i.e. a silent pass), mirroring the warning `get_ci_checks()` already logs for an unmapped `_GH_STATE_MAP` key; an unrecognized check-run `status` or commit-status `state` is logged and defaults to `IN_PROGRESS` (never completed), which is already safe since it cannot be mistaken for a pass. (5) Elapsed time inside the poll loop is measured from `time.monotonic()` at loop entry, not by counting `poll_interval` increments, since each iteration can now cost up to four API round trips (SHA-scoped lookup, the no-checks cross-check, the head-mismatch check, the unknown-commit PR-head cross-check) where the old PR-level lookup made one; counting intervals let that drift exceed the step's fixed 120s timeout grace (`ci.max_wait + 120`) and get killed as an uninformative `step_hard_timeout` instead of returning the clean "CI monitoring timed out" result the fix loop expects. (6) A fully completed check set with nothing in it that is `is_failed` is not automatically a pass: the normal consequence of a superseding push is GitHub Actions concurrency cancelling the polled commit's runs, and `CANCELLED` is neither `is_passed` nor `is_failed`. `_poll_ci()` and `_check_ci_on_resume()` both return a named failure, instead of silently reading as green, for an all-completed set with no `is_passed` check **and** at least one `CANCELLED` conclusion present. The guard is scoped to `CANCELLED` specifically, not to "nothing passed" generally: an all-`SKIPPED` or all-`NEUTRAL` completed set (the normal result of a project's CI skipping every job via path filters, e.g. for a docs-only PR) is a deliberate non-blocking outcome and still reports success, matching the pre-existing `is_failed`/`is_passed` classification for those conclusions. Separately, `CICheck.is_failed` now treats `ACTION_REQUIRED` as blocking (affecting every `get_ci_checks`/`get_ci_checks_for_sha` consumer, not just this step), `_GH_STATE_MAP` (backing `get_ci_checks()`) carries the matching `"ACTION_REQUIRED"` entry so both lookup paths classify it identically, and both the check-runs and commit-status REST calls share one pagination helper (`_fetch_remaining_pages()`) past their first 100 entries, returning `None` (not a truncated list) if the shared `_MAX_CHECK_PAGES` page cap is hit, since a partial result could report "all completed" while an unfetched check is still pending or failing. `agent_recovery.py`'s own `_summarize_ci_checks()` (used by the dashboard's PR-status/resolver path, independent of `MonitorCIStep`) delegates to `CICheck.is_failed`/`is_completed` rather than comparing conclusions directly, so it cannot drift from `MonitorCIStep`'s classification the way it did when `ACTION_REQUIRED` and `TIMED_OUT` both fell through to its own `"passed"` default. It mirrors the same `CANCELLED`-specific carve-out as `_poll_ci()`: a check set with no `is_passed` entry and at least one `CANCELLED` conclusion reports `"failed"` rather than falling through to `"passed"`, since that string feeds `PRFacts.ci_status` and would otherwise let a superseded, never-actually-green push unlock Integrate. Files: `sova/git/pr.py`, `sova/core/steps/monitor_ci.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_recovery.py`. Issue #1175.
- **Guard against no-op pushes in LLM fix loops**: after invoking the LLM to fix code (CI failures, lint errors), verify a new commit was created before pushing. Use `git rev-list --count origin/{branch}..HEAD`: if 0, the LLM edited the working tree but didn't commit, or produced no changes at all. Without this guard, the push sends stale HEAD, CI sees the same failure, and the loop wastes an attempt. File: `sova/core/steps/monitor_ci.py:_try_fix_ci()`.
- **Fix-loop LLM timeouts get a distinct marker, and the inner check loop stops before the outer step timeout kills it uncleanly, without stealing the runaway guard's pause/resume path**: measurement against this repo's own `.claude/sova.db` (last 30 days) showed the developer timeout cluster is dominated by a single first-cycle fix-LLM timeout arriving after `DevelopStep` had already spent most of `develop.step_timeout` on the initial `/develop` call plus one check run, not by a non-convergent loop repeating the same fix (`_check_duplicate_failure()` almost never fires in production). Two independent fixes address this. (1) `format_fix_llm_failure(exc, cycle)` in `sova/llm/errors.py`, reusing `LLMTimeoutError` from the provider boundary rather than inventing a second classification scheme, prefixes `fix_llm_timeout on cycle N: ...` for a timed-out fix invocation and `fix_llm_failed on cycle N: ...` otherwise, so `StepExecution.error_message`/`FailureRecord.message` can distinguish a fix-LLM timeout from a non-timeout fix-LLM failure and from the unrelated `step_hard_timeout` string (`WorkflowEngine._run_step_with_timeout()`) without regex-guessing over free text. Applied at all three fix-loop catch sites: `DevelopStep._invoke_fix_llm()`, `ValidateStep.execute()`'s hook-fix retry, `MonitorCIStep._try_fix_ci()`. (2) `WorkflowEngine._run_step_with_timeout()` mirrors the step's own effective hard timeout onto `ExecutionContext.step_started_at`/`step_deadline_seconds` immediately before `step.execute()` runs (same pattern as `run_started_at`, 0.0/None meaning "no WorkflowEngine involved"), exposed via the `ctx.step_time_remaining` property. `DevelopStep._check_loop_budget()` uses it to bail out before starting a cycle whose worst case (`develop.check_timeout + develop.fix_timeout`, plus a fixed `_CYCLE_OVERHEAD_BUFFER_SECONDS` allowance for the non-LLM overhead between them: git diff/status calls, ruff, commit) cannot fit inside what remains of the step's own deadline, returning a clean `"... (step deadline approaching)"` summary instead of letting the outer `asyncio.timeout` kill the step mid-cycle and report the uninformative `step_hard_timeout`, which also starves whatever pipeline steps run after `develop` in the same run. This preemptive bail-out is skipped whenever `ExecutionContext.step_deadline_is_runaway` is `True` (mirrored alongside `step_deadline_seconds` from `_effective_step_timeout()`'s `capped_by_runaway` return value): when the run-wide `runaway.max_run_wall_clock_seconds` guard, not the step's own configured timeout, is what capped the step's deadline, bailing out here would return a plain failed `StepResult` and silently lose `WorkflowEngine`'s `runaway_triggered`/`_pause_for_runaway` (resumable PAUSED) classification, downgrading it to an ordinary FAILED result. Deliberately does not attempt mid-cycle interruption (each `invoke()`/`run()` call already bounds itself) and does not change `fix_timeout`/`max_fix_time`/`step_timeout` values themselves: the fix is entirely about awareness and reporting, not new budgets. Files: `sova/llm/errors.py`, `sova/core/context.py`, `sova/core/workflow.py`, `sova/core/steps/develop.py`, `sova/core/steps/validate.py`, `sova/core/steps/monitor_ci.py`. Issue #977.
- **Failure capture must prefer stdout's structured error over stderr, and a bare exit code is never enough to triage**: two capture sites silently discarded the real cause of an agent failure. (1) `_extract_failure_detail()` in `sova/llm/providers/claude_code.py` returned `result.stderr[:500]` immediately whenever stderr was non-empty, before ever looking at stdout, even though `--output-format json` writes the real error (`is_error`/`terminal_reason`/`result`) to stdout while stderr often carries only an incidental warning (e.g. a model-availability notice the CLI emits regardless of the configured model). `_parse_stdout_error()` is now checked first and wins whenever it finds a structured payload; `_strip_leading_warnings()` strips a contiguous prefix of `Warning:` lines from stderr before it is used as a fallback (matched case-insensitively on the *stripped* line, so an indented or lowercase `warning:` is still recognized), so a warning can never masquerade as the cause, and an explicit `"(no error detail captured beyond warnings)"` is returned when nothing else remains. (2) `_finalize_task_run()` in `sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py` set `error_message` to the bare `f"Process exited with code {exit_code}"` for the `_wait_and_finalize()` path (a spawned process exiting nonzero with no structured `LLMError` raised). `_build_exit_failure_message()` now appends a bounded tail of the run's captured output (`AgentState.output_lines`, last 20 lines, taken via `itertools.islice` over a reversed view rather than materializing the whole `deque(maxlen=5000)`) and the last recorded step name (`TaskRun.current_step`, skipped when still the `"agent"` sentinel), assembled as a single line (`Process exited with code {n} (step={name}); last output: {tail}`, tail lines joined by `" | "`) so the value stays readable in table cells and log lines, with the whole message capped at 2000 chars so concatenating already-truncated fragments can never bloat the row. The output tail is raw captured agent stdout/stderr, so it is redacted via `scan_and_redact()` (`sova/llm/egress.py`) on the *full* joined text before the 500-char tail slice is taken, not after: redacting after truncation could slice a secret in half at the boundary and leave a mangled fragment that no longer matches the redaction regex. Each of the 20 raw lines is additionally capped to `_OUTPUT_LINE_MAX_CHARS` (2000) before joining, independent of and prior to that redact-then-truncate step: a single oversized line (e.g. a stream-json text block with no internal newlines) would otherwise reach the redaction regex unbounded, and since `_build_exit_failure_message()` runs inside `_finalize_task_run()`'s `session.begin()` block, an expensive scan over a huge string would hold the DB write lock for its duration. `_extract_failure_detail()`'s structured-stdout branch caps the fully joined `"; ".join(stdout_parts)` to 500 chars (not just the `result` field's existing 300-char sub-cap), since an unusually long `terminal_reason` would otherwise bypass every other length limit in the function. Unlike the full per-line output (DB-only, dashboard log viewer), `error_message` is also pushed to an external telemetry hub by `telemetry_push.py` whenever `[telemetry]` is configured, so an unredacted tail would leak secrets off-machine. Neither change touches `TaskRun.status` or the terminal-failure classification in `_validate_command_outcome`/`_validate_pipeline_outcome`/`rollback_issue_state`, which key off status, not message text. Files: `sova/llm/providers/claude_code.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`, `tests/test_agent_failure_capture.py`, `tests/test_llm.py`. Issue #975.
- **Address-review pipeline needs independent worktree discovery**: the address-review pipeline may run on a branch whose worktree was created by a prior developer run. Worktrees can be lost (cleanup, pruning, project rename). `DeveloperRole._discover_address_review_context()` checks `.claude/worktrees/{issue_number}`, then falls back to `find_worktree_by_branch()` when the issue-based directory doesn't exist (guards against returning project_dir as worktree). It also discovers `branch_name` from the PR's `headRefName` via `get_pr_branch()`. All three are defense-in-depth: the reviewer should propagate `branch_name` in its handoff, but the developer self-discovers if it's missing. `start_agent()` in `agent_lifecycle.py` resolves `issue` from `pr_number` via `_resolve_issue_from_pr()` when issue is empty, and resolves the worktree via `_resolve_issue_worktree()` using the PR's branch name: matching `start_command()`'s pattern. DB operations (`_create_task_run`, `OutputWriter`, lifecycle) always use `project_dir`; only the subprocess spawn uses the worktree `cwd`. Files: `sova/roles/developer.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_lifecycle.py`.
- **An unresolvable `branch_name` must fail loudly, not silently reach `git push`**: a pre-existing worktree at `.claude/worktrees/pr-{N}` (left over from a prior developer run) let `EnsureWorktreeStep.can_skip()` return `True` even when `get_pr_branch()` had silently returned `""` (no logging at the time), so `ctx.branch_name` stayed empty through the whole address-review pipeline and `PushStep` shelled out to `git push -u origin  --force-with-lease` (note the double space), failing with an uninformative `fatal: invalid refspec ''`. `get_pr_branch()` now logs a `git.get_pr_branch.failed` warning with the PR number, a `reason` (`gh_command_failed`/`json_decode_failed`/`missing_head_ref_name`), and the relevant `stderr`/`stdout` snippet for every failure path, matching this file's other command-failure warnings. `_discover_address_review_context()` raises `RuntimeError` when `branch_name` is still empty after all fallback attempts (worktree discovery stays independent and unchanged), and `DeveloperRole._execute_address_review()` catches it and returns a failed `RoleResult` rather than letting it propagate out of `execute()`. `PushStep.execute()` and `sova/git/branch.py:push()` both add an explicit `if not branch` guard as defense in depth, so any other caller of `push()` gets the same protection even if a future code path bypasses the role-level check. Issue #1016.
- **Force-push decisions come from a reachability check, not the `ctx.pr_number` proxy**: `PushStep` used to force-with-lease whenever `ctx.pr_number` was set, which is wrong in both directions: an address-review run with a PR number force-pushes even when a plain fast-forward push would do (masking a case where someone else has genuinely moved the branch), and a fresh developer run with no PR yet never forces even after its own local rebase has rewritten history and made HEAD a non-descendant of a stale local state. `check_branch_divergence()` (`sova/git/branch.py`) answers the real question: it fetches `origin/{branch}` fresh into `FETCH_HEAD` (not a possibly-stale remote-tracking ref, since the worktree may never have fetched this branch before) and runs `git merge-base --is-ancestor <remote_sha> HEAD`, returning one of `NO_REMOTE_REF` (first push of a new branch; a fetch failure specifically naming a missing ref, matched by substring since git's wording varies), `ANCESTOR` (remote is reachable from HEAD; a plain push suffices), `DIVERGED` (remote is not reachable from HEAD; force-with-lease is required), or `ERROR` (fetch/rev-parse/merge-base failed for an unrelated reason, e.g. a shallow clone truncating history). Only `DIVERGED` forces, and always with `lease_sha=divergence.remote_sha` so `push()` sends `--force-with-lease=<branch>:<sha>` (added as an optional `lease_sha` parameter alongside the pre-existing bare `--force-with-lease` fallback used by `MonitorCIStep`'s and `AddressExternalFindingsStep`'s own `git_ops.push(force=True)` call sites, both unchanged and out of this scope): a concurrent writer that has moved origin past the observed SHA gets the push refused rather than silently overwritten. `ERROR` and `NO_REMOTE_REF` both fall through to a plain push in `PushStep` (preserving prior behavior when git is unreachable, and the normal path for a brand-new branch) rather than guessing. A push still rejected (`is_push_rejection()` matches `non-fast-forward`, `fetch first`, `stale info`, and git's untagged "updates were rejected because the remote contains work" hint, case-insensitively; deliberately excludes the bare word "rejected" alone, since a pre-receive hook declining a push for secret scanning or branch protection commonly phrases its message that way too, and misclassifying that as a fast-forward/lease conflict would trigger a wasted re-check and an unwarranted retry) is re-evaluated once, but only when the attempt that was just rejected had already decided to force (the original divergence read was already `DIVERGED`). A plain push (`ANCESTOR`) that gets rejected is never retried into a force: that rejection means a concurrent writer landed a real commit between the check and the push, and re-deriving `force` from that now-conflicting state would produce a lease trivially satisfied by the very commit that caused the rejection, silently discarding it. **Being in the `DIVERGED` branch already is not itself sufficient**: re-deriving the lease from a fresh `check_branch_divergence()` call on the retry has exactly the same defect if the remote moved again during the race window, since that fresh read observes the very commit that rejected the first attempt and would trivially satisfy a lease built from it. `PushStep.execute()` therefore pins `first_lease` from the first attempt's `_resolve_force()` call and compares the retry's freshly-observed lease against it before ever calling `_push()` again: an unchanged lease means the rejection was transient (a client/network hiccup, not an actual remote move) and the retry proceeds with that same lease; a lease that has moved means a genuine concurrent writer landed a commit in the race window, and the retry aborts with a clear error naming both SHAs instead of pushing at all. Only that transient-and-unchanged case can reach a second rejection, which surfaces as a clear step failure, never a third attempt. This closes a gap both SOVA's own review and CodeRabbit found in the first cut of this mechanism (PR #1080): comparing only "was the original decision DIVERGED" was not enough to prevent the DIVERGED branch from silently discarding a concurrent writer's commit on retry, exactly the "concurrent writer" edge case this mechanism exists to protect against. `PushStep._log_dropped_commits()` lists the discarded commits via `git log --oneline {head}..{remote}` (capped at 20 entries with a `"... and N more"` suffix before it reaches the log line or feed `detail`, matching this file's other truncate-before-log/telemetry conventions) and emits a feed event through `emit_safe()` (lazy-imported inside the function, matching `sova/core/dag.py`'s pattern, so `sova/core/steps/` carries no module-level dashboard dependency): `warning` severity when commits are actually discarded, `info` when the list is empty. This only fires after `git_ops.push()` has actually returned successfully, using the divergence snapshot from the attempt that succeeded rather than the one that decided to force: emitting on the decision instead of the confirmed outcome would announce discarded commits for a push a concurrent writer had just refused, and could name the wrong SHAs if a first-attempt rejection is followed by a second, differently-diverged read; `_resolve_force()` itself is a pure decision function with no logging or side effects for exactly this reason. `AssessStep` runs the identical `check_branch_divergence()` check at the very start of `execute()`, gated on both `ctx.branch_name` and `ctx.worktree_dir` being set (a no-op for a fresh developer run before `CreateWorktreeStep` sets either): `ExecutionContext.working_dir` falls back to `project_dir` (the main checkout) whenever `worktree_dir` is `None`, and a resumed run whose persisted `worktree_path` no longer exists on disk restores `branch_name` without a live worktree, so guarding on `branch_name` alone would run `git fetch`/`rev-parse HEAD` against the main checkout's `HEAD` instead of the actual feature branch; the check is skipped (logged) rather than guessed at in that case. When it does run, a branch a human has already diverged from fails in seconds with both SHAs named in the error, instead of the pipeline discovering the same conflict at `PushStep` after a full develop cycle. Neither step attempts to auto-resolve a real `DIVERGED` result: recovery (rebase vs. force) is left to a human or the supervisor. Files: `sova/git/branch.py`, `sova/git/operations.py`, `sova/core/steps/push.py`, `sova/core/steps/assess.py`. Issue #1078.
- **`WorkflowEngine` DB session calls resolve `project_dir` explicitly, not via the ambient contextvar**: while diagnosing the branch_name discovery bug above, every `get_session()` call inside `WorkflowEngine` (12 call sites: `_check_per_issue_budget`, `_create_task_run`, `_adopt_task_run`, `_set_current_step`, `_update_task_run_status`, `_sync_task_run_context`, `_finalize_task_run`, `_update_step_execution_status`, `_create_step_execution`, `_update_step_execution`, `_update_step_execution_gate`, `_record_failure`) was calling `get_session()` with no argument, which falls back to `sova.config.context.get_project_dir()` (the per-request contextvar used by multi-project dashboard mode) whenever it is set, and only to the global default DB otherwise. `WorkflowEngine` already holds the authoritative project directory on `self._ctx.project_dir` (established once at construction from the role/CLI invocation, per "Subprocess agents must resolve project root from linked worktree CWD" above), so relying on the contextvar instead was a latent correctness gap: a stale or differently-scoped contextvar value (e.g. a dashboard request for a different project completing concurrently, or the contextvar simply never being set for a CLI-spawned run) would silently divert a run's TaskRun/StepExecution/FailureRecord writes to the wrong project's database rather than the one it was actually invoked for. All 12 call sites now pass `self._ctx.project_dir` explicitly, so `WorkflowEngine`'s DB routing is independent of whatever the contextvar happens to hold. `test_db_writes_use_explicit_project_dir_not_ambient_contextvar` in `tests/test_core.py` is the regression test: it points the contextvar at a different project directory than `ctx.project_dir` and confirms the resulting `TaskRun` lands in `ctx.project_dir`'s database only. File: `sova/core/workflow.py`.
- **Supervisor auto-rebase must reuse a branch's existing worktree, never collide with it**: `attempt_auto_rebase()` (the "Rebase" dashboard button and `SPAWN_REBASE`) always created a dedicated `rebase-pr-{pr_number}` worktree for the PR branch. Git refuses to check out the same branch into two worktrees at once, and a PR's original developer worktree at `.claude/worktrees/{issue_number}` typically still exists while the PR is open, so every rebase attempt on such a PR failed with `fatal: '<branch>' is already used by worktree at ...`. `_resolve_rebase_worktree()` (the worktree-selection logic extracted out of `attempt_auto_rebase()` to keep its own cognitive complexity down) calls `find_worktree_by_branch()` first (the same discovery pattern `EnsureWorktreeStep` and `DeveloperRole` use), after running `git worktree prune` so a stale registration for a deleted worktree directory is never mistaken for a live one. Three outcomes fork from the lookup: (1) the branch is checked out in the main project checkout itself (`existing_wt.resolve() == project_dir.resolve()`, no dedicated worktree exists), skipped cleanly rather than reused or collided into a second `create_worktree()` call, since both would fail or risk the main checkout the way `/address-pr`'s own worktree-safety rule warns against; (2) no worktree is registered at all, so a dedicated `rebase-pr-{pr_number}` worktree is created; (3) the branch is checked out in some other, non-main worktree, reused instead of creating a second one, and the caller skips the `cleanup_worktree()` call in `finally` since it does not own a worktree it did not create. Before reusing in case (3), it calls `check_worktree_active_agent()` (promoted from the private `_check_worktree_active_agent`, which `resolve_worktree_conflict()` already used) and fails closed: an actively-used worktree is never touched, and the rebase is reported as skipped/busy instead of racing a running agent. It then also checks `_worktree_is_clean()` (`git status --porcelain`) before reuse: `rebase_with_conflict_resolution()` stashes and pops uncommitted changes around the rebase, so reusing a dirty worktree risks stranding a developer's in-progress edits in the shared stash if the pop ever fails; an unreadable status also fails closed (treated as dirty) rather than assumed clean. Two more hardenings close out the remaining gaps: (1) every safety-skip finalize call (`branch_in_main_worktree`, the active-agent safety-check failure, `worktree_busy`, and the dirty-or-unreadable check) passes `_finalize_run(..., attempted=False)`, and `_already_attempted()` only treats a matching `head_sha` record as a real attempt when `handoff_json.get("attempted", True)` is true; without this, a transient skip (a worktree busy five minutes ago, now idle) would permanently block every later retry at that HEAD SHA, since nothing else ever changes it. Records written before this flag existed have no `attempted` key and default to `True` (a real attempt), so old skip records are not silently reinterpreted. (2) `attempt_auto_rebase()` is called directly, with no other coordination, from both the dashboard's `trigger_rebase` endpoint and the progression daemon's `SPAWN_REBASE` path, and `_already_attempted()`'s check-then-`_create_rebase_run()` is not atomic, so two concurrent calls for the same PR could both pass the worktree-reuse checks and race inside `_run_rebase_and_push()`. A module-level `_rebase_locks: dict[int, asyncio.Lock]` keyed by `pr_number` (mirroring the identity-keyed tracker pattern in `github_quota.py`, never evicted) is acquired around the `_already_attempted()` check through the `_run_rebase_and_push()` return, so a second concurrent call for the same PR blocks until the first fully finishes (success, failure, or safety skip) rather than reusing the same worktree at the same time; both callers get this transparently since the lock lives inside `attempt_auto_rebase()` itself. A third review round caught that the lock's own `async with` block only wrapped the happy path, not the exception handling: `attempt_auto_rebase()`'s original try/except/finally wrapped the whole function including the `async with` block, so `__aexit__` (releasing the lock) ran before the outer `except Exception` finalized the `TaskRun` and the outer `finally` cleaned up an owned worktree, leaving a window where a waiting same-PR call could acquire the lock and start reusing that worktree while the failed call was still tearing it down. `asyncio.CancelledError` was also invisible to `except Exception` (it is a `BaseException` subclass since Python 3.8), so a cancelled run released the lock via the `async with` block without ever finalizing its `TaskRun`, leaving it stuck at `status="running"` forever. Fixed by extracting `_run_locked_rebase()` (the already-attempted-check through rebase-and-push flow, including its own try/`except asyncio.CancelledError`/`except Exception`/`finally`) and calling it entirely inside `attempt_auto_rebase()`'s `async with _get_rebase_lock(pr_number):` block, so finalize and cleanup both complete before the lock's `__aexit__` runs; the `CancelledError` branch finalizes the run as failed and re-raises (never swallowing cancellation). `attempt_auto_rebase()` keeps a separate, lock-free try/except around config load and PR lookup, since no `TaskRun` exists yet at that point to finalize. Files: `sova/supervisor/rebase.py`, `sova/git/worktree.py`.
- **Address-review finding loading uses four fallback sources**: `AddressReviewStep` reads findings from: (1) handoff file (`handoff.json`), (2) resumed run's DB handoff (`resume_run_id`), (3) most recent reviewer run by issue number (`_load_review_findings_by_issue()`), (4) GitHub PR review bodies via `_load_findings_from_github_reviews()` (covers reviews posted as body comments that handoff-based loaders miss, using `ctx.adapter.get_pr_reviews()` for the API call). The third fallback is essential when the handoff file is cleared by auto-handoff and `resume_run_id` points to a non-reviewer run. Also fetches CodeRabbit findings from the PR via `_load_coderabbit_findings()` and merges them into the prompt. File: `sova/core/steps/address_review.py`.
- **External review thread lifecycle**: CodeRabbit's `CHANGES_REQUESTED` review state persists across force-pushes: new commits do NOT auto-dismiss it. After addressing bot findings: (1) reply to each inline comment with the fix or acknowledgement, (2) resolve each conversation thread via `resolveReviewThread` GraphQL mutation, (3) dismiss the `CHANGES_REQUESTED` review via `PUT /reviews/{id}/dismissals` (filter on `user.type == "Bot"`: never dismiss human reviews). Steps 2 and 3 are separate API operations: dismissing the review does NOT resolve its threads, and resolving threads does NOT dismiss the review. Both must be done or the PR shows either "unresolved conversations" or "changes requested" blockers. The fix-before-reply order is critical: push fixed code first, then reply and resolve: otherwise CodeRabbit re-reviews unfixed code and re-posts `CHANGES_REQUESTED`. Verify `gh auth status` matches the configured `github_user` (`sova config get github_user`) before thread operations; mismatched accounts get FORBIDDEN. Thread resolution for bot reviewers differs from human reviewers: resolve both Fixed AND Acknowledged bot threads (no human confirmation needed); for human threads only resolve Fixed ones. File: `.claude/commands/address-pr.md` (steps 11-12).
- **address-pr wrong-directory failure mode**: when `_resolve_issue_worktree()` falls back to `project_dir` (e.g., no worktree exists for a manually-developed PR branch), the command agent's CWD is the main repo. If the agent then runs `git checkout <HEAD_BRANCH>`, it switches the main repo to the PR branch. Any uncommitted changes from the run bleed onto `main` when the agent cleans up with `git checkout main`. The `/address-pr` command guards against this: step 4 requires `git branch --show-current` to match `headRefName` before proceeding; if no matching worktree exists, the command stops rather than using `git checkout`. Step 10 (squash/reset) adds a second guard verifying the branch before any `git reset`.
- **address-pr own-PR and push-access guard**: step 3 of `/address-pr` runs before any worktree or commit work, since a run that gets this far unauthorized would fix code it has no right to push. It checks, in order: (1) the active `gh` account matches the project's configured `github_user`, switching first if not, since comparing identity against the wrong account produces a false "not your PR"; (2) the authenticated login equals the PR's `author.login` (skipped, with the skip stated explicitly in the guard summary, only when `gh api user` fails on the app-token-unavailable condition, matched on its message, `"Resource not accessible by integration"`, or a bare 404; any other failure, including a rate-limited 403 or a network error, STOPs rather than silently skipping the check, since "could not verify" must never be treated as "could not find"); (3) the PR is `OPEN`; (4) the head repository still exists; (5) push access, computed from the base repo's permissions for a same-repo PR, or the fork's permissions OR'd with (`maintainerCanModify` AND base-repo push) for a fork PR. Step 4e adds a non-destructive confirmation once the correct worktree and branch are checked out: `git push --dry-run --no-verify --force-with-lease` against the branch's own configured remote (not hardcoded to `origin`, since a fork PR's push target differs) exercises the actual credential (SSH key, credential helper) the real push in step 12 will use, which can differ from the `gh` OAuth token the API permission check above reads. Because the branch is already in sync with its remote at this point, the dry-run proposes no ref update, so it proves only that the credential can reach `git-receive-pack`: a branch-protection rule is evaluated at ref-update time and so still surfaces only at the real push in step 12. `--no-verify` skips the local pre-push hook here deliberately, since this check runs before findings are fixed and would otherwise STOP on the very issues the command exists to fix. A guard STOP must end the run as a failure, not a clean summary: the supervisor's address-cycle counter only increments on a posted `sova-addressed` marker (step 15), so a STOP that reports cleanly looks like "nothing to do" and the supervisor keeps respawning `/address-pr` on the same PR every poll. Reporting it as a failed run at least surfaces the repetition in run history, but `check_repeated_failures_gate()` does not bound it: that gate only fires for the `researcher` and `developer` roles, never for `command:address-pr`, so today only the `already_running` concurrency guard prevents a second simultaneous spawn and nothing caps the respawn count on an unpushable or foreign PR. File: `sova/commands/data/commands/address-pr.md` (step 3).
- **`.claude/scripts/ci-failures.sh` project hook contract**: an optional, project-supplied executable (same convention as `.claude/scripts/prepush-check.sh` in `sova/commands/data/commands/pr.md`) that `/address-pr` and `/integrate-pr` try first when a CI check fails, since GitHub Actions is not the only CI backend a project may run. Contract: invoked as `.claude/scripts/ci-failures.sh <PR_NUMBER>`, prints failure text for the PR's failing checks to stdout, and its own exit code is not interpreted (a non-zero exit or empty stdout both mean "no evidence from this source", falling through to reading the GitHub Actions log directly, and then to reporting just the check name and link). `/address-pr` resolves the hook from the primary checkout (`git rev-parse --git-common-dir`), not a bare relative path, since this command usually runs inside a per-issue worktree where an untracked project hook would never be found. No project in this repository ships one yet; the hook exists as an extension point for projects whose CI is not GitHub Actions.
- **Verify `gh auth` account before review thread operations**: `resolveReviewThread` GraphQL mutation and review dismissal API return FORBIDDEN if the active `gh` account lacks write access to the repo. Before replying to or resolving PR threads, verify the active account matches the configured `github_user` (`sova config get github_user`) via `gh auth status`. Switch with `gh auth switch --user <target>` if needed.
- **Headless agent autonomy**: `ClaudeCodeRuntime.spawn()` prepends a `_HEADLESS_PREAMBLE` to every prompt and passes `--permission-mode bypassPermissions`. In Claude CLI's `-p` mode, a text-only `end_turn` (no tool call) causes the process to exit: so if the model asks "May I proceed?" instead of acting, the agent dies mid-task. The preamble instructs the model to act without confirmation; `bypassPermissions` fully bypasses the permission system (`auto` only auto-approves tools already in the settings.json allowlist, which is insufficient for headless agents that need to edit arbitrary project files). File: `sova/ipc/runtime.py`. Corollary: explicit command step instructions ("Ask the user before posting") override the preamble because they are more specific and closer to the action. Commands that may run headlessly must never contain approval gates, confirmation prompts, or "show draft first" language in any step. Audit commands for such language before shipping. PR #404.
- **Direct subprocess spawn for pipeline roles**: `start_agent()` in `agent_lifecycle.py` uses `spawn_direct()` from `sova/ipc/runtime.py` for pipeline roles (`developer`, `researcher`, `planner`). This spawns `sova run` directly via `asyncio.create_subprocess_exec`, bypassing the Claude Code intermediary. Eliminates the 600s Bash tool timeout that killed long-running develop steps, and saves ~$0.50/run wrapper cost. Non-pipeline roles (`reviewer`, custom) and `start_command()` still go through `ClaudeCodeRuntime.spawn()` since they need Claude's LLM capabilities. `_PIPELINE_ROLES = frozenset({"developer", "researcher", "planner"})` in `runtime.py` controls the split. The output parser (`_parse_stream_line`) handles plain text from `sova run` via JSON fallback; cost tracking comes from the DB (WorkflowEngine), not the stream. Files: `sova/ipc/runtime.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_lifecycle.py`.
- **Two CLI invocation paths share one argv builder, and the prompt is never on that argv**: `ClaudeCodeRuntime.spawn()` (dashboard-spawned non-pipeline agents via `sova/ipc/runtime.py`) and `ClaudeCodeProvider.invoke()`/`invoke_streaming()` (WorkflowEngine step invocations via `sova/llm/providers/claude_code.py`) both run `claude -p` headlessly. Both build their argv via `build_claude_cli_args()` in `sova/llm/cli_args.py`, a module with no intra-project imports of its own, so it can never be the module that closes an import cycle between the IPC and provider layers (it is not import-free: `import sova.llm.cli_args` still runs `sova/llm/__init__`, which pulls in `client` and `provider`). `--verbose` is added automatically whenever `output_format="stream-json"` (the Claude CLI requires it alongside `-p` plus `--output-format stream-json`), so both streaming call sites get it uniformly. `_build_args` in the provider module is a thin re-export of `build_claude_cli_args`, kept under its historical name because `tests/test_llm.py` and `tests/test_model_fallback_cli.py` import it by that name. `build_claude_cli_args()` takes no `prompt` parameter: `-p` reads from stdin instead (the default `--input-format text` mode), because a prompt sitting on argv is visible to any `ps`/`/proc/pid/cmdline` reader and can be accidentally matched by an unrelated `pkill -f <pattern>` whose pattern happens to appear inside prompt text, killing a headless Claude process that has nothing to do with the intended target (observed in production against a `pkill -f pytest` call that matched a prompt containing the word "pytest"). Every caller writes the prompt to the spawned process's stdin itself, via `write_stdin_and_close()` in `sova/utils/shell.py`: `_spawn_agent_process()`/`_spawn_with_file_output()` in `runtime.py` accept a `stdin_payload` kwarg (the runtime applies `_HEADLESS_PREAMBLE` to the prompt before passing it as that payload, still a prompt transformation kept out of the shared argv builder), `ClaudeCodeProvider.invoke()` passes the prompt through `run()`'s pre-existing `stdin=` kwarg (piped via `communicate()`, safe against the write/read pipe-buffer deadlock that a manual write would risk), and `_start_streaming_process()` calls `write_stdin_and_close()` directly on the live process handle it returns for `invoke_streaming()`. `write_stdin_and_close()` writes and closes (EOF) under a bounded `_STDIN_WRITE_TIMEOUT_SECONDS` (30s). A broken pipe or a stalled drain kills and reaps the child (`_kill_and_reap()`, mirroring `run()`'s own timeout-kill pattern in the same module) rather than leaving it running on a truncated prompt, then logs and returns rather than raising, so the caller still reaches its own exit-code/stderr handling for the real diagnostic against an already-dead process. Cancellation of the awaiting task is handled differently: the child is killed and reaped the same way, but `CancelledError` is always re-raised, never swallowed, because at that point in every call site the process wrapper (`AgentProcess`/`FileAgentProcess`/the raw `proc`) has not yet been constructed or returned, so this is the only place that can prevent the already-spawned child from leaking untracked. On CLI failure, the provider's error handler must check stdout JSON (not just stderr) because `--output-format json` routes error details to stdout. Pipeline roles bypass both paths via `spawn_direct()`. The system prompt gets the same treatment where it applies: `ClaudeCodeProvider.invoke()` is the only call site that accepts one (`invoke_streaming()`'s `system_prompt` parameter is dead code, never forwarded to `_start_streaming_process()`; the IPC runtime spawn path never accepts one at all), and passing it as `--system-prompt <text>` would reopen the same argv-visibility gap for exactly the kind of caller most likely to need it: `sova/supervisor/planner.py` formats the user-editable operations persona into its system prompt. `write_system_prompt_file()` (`sova/llm/cli_args.py`) writes the text to a private `tempfile.mkstemp` file (mode 0600) and `build_claude_cli_args()` passes `--system-prompt-file <path>` instead; `invoke()` deletes the file in a `finally` block once the subprocess has exited.
- **Pipeline step invocations are task_type-tagged for config-driven routing**: every `invoke()`/`invoke_command()` call site in `sova/core/steps/`, `sova/roles/`, `sova/supervisor/planner.py`, and `sova/git/rebase.py`'s single-model fallback path passes a `task_type=` keyword (e.g. `"develop"`, `"develop_fix"`, `"validate"`, `"monitor_ci"`, `"review"`, `"triage"`, `"pr_body"`, `"rebase"`, `"address_review"`) so `llm.routing.{task_type}` in config can steer any step to a different model (including local/Ollama offloading) without code changes. **Tagging is live selection**: PR4 (#913) flipped `_resolve_task_type_model()` so a configured `llm.routing[task_type]` route outranks the explicit `model=` every pipeline step passes (`model=ctx.resolved_model or ctx.config.agent.model`); the explicit model is only the fallback when no route is configured for that key. Under the default empty `llm.routing`, no key matches and behavior is unchanged from having no routing at all, but setting `llm.routing.develop` does reroute the develop step today. `sova/llm/routing.py:TASK_TYPE_KEYS` is a registry of known keys for settings-UI/validation consumers, not enforced by `route_model()` itself. Two call sites are deliberately left untagged: `sova/cli/commands/pr.py` (dynamic user-invoked CLI command) and `sova/mcp/tools.py` (caller-supplied arbitrary command) inherit client-level model fallback with no task_type routing by design, each documented by a comment in place (see `docs/model-selection-risk-assessment.md` section 4). One call site deliberately bypasses the client abstraction entirely and so accepts a bare `model=` positional/keyword without task_type: `sova/git/rebase.py`'s multi-model consensus fan-out (per-model `LiteLLM` providers, tracked under R14), now gated on `sova.llm.backends.detect_backend() is Backend.FIRSTPARTY` (a route check, not just `is_anthropic_capable()`: `llm.provider="vertex"` and a Vertex/Bedrock-redirected `claude-code` CLI are both Anthropic-capable yet neither is FIRSTPARTY, and the bare, unprefixed consensus model IDs would still misroute to the direct Anthropic API under either) so its Anthropic-by-convention model IDs are dropped rather than silently reaching Anthropic under a non-FIRSTPARTY route (#924). `sova/dashboard/services/llm_suggestion_service.py` was the second such site until #924 moved it onto `create_provider()`, and a later pass moved it again onto `sova.llm.client.invoke()` itself (so its cost is recorded and it counts against the runaway-call guard); it passes `task_type="pr_suggestion"` (unrouted under the default empty `llm.routing`) and `isolated=True`. `tests/test_model_literal_guard.py` is an AST-based guard (not regex, to avoid false positives on docstrings/log lines/test fixtures) that fails the suite if any `invoke`/`invoke_command`/`invoke_streaming` call site under `sova/` passes a bare string literal as `model=`, resolving both attribute-form callees (`client.invoke(...)`) and aliased imports (`from sova.llm.client import invoke as llm_invoke`). The guard has no allowlist: every prerequisite PR has landed, so no bare literal remains under `sova/` and any new one is a real violation.
- **Headless agent prompts must frame CLI commands as bash code blocks**: applies only to non-pipeline roles spawned via `ClaudeCodeRuntime.spawn()`. When `start_agent()` sends `sova run` to Claude Code for reviewer/custom roles, the command is wrapped in bash framing (`Run the following command...` + fenced ```bash block) with `_HEADLESS_PREAMBLE` to prevent the model from interpreting it as a task. Pipeline roles (developer, researcher, planner) bypass this entirely via `spawn_direct()`. Also use `shlex.quote()` on string tokens for defense-in-depth. Files: `sova/ipc/runtime.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_lifecycle.py`.
- **Dashboard JS polling must clear stale UI on negative path**: when JS polls an API for transient state (running agents, pending handoffs, batch progress), the negative path (state gone) must actively clear the UI element's `innerHTML` or add `hidden`, not just `return`. If state changes between polls (agent finishes, handoff is dismissed), orphaned panels persist until page reload. Pattern: iterate all containers at the top of the poll handler and clear when the state is absent. Applies to: `loadAgents()` (agents-grid), `checkStandaloneHandoff()` (handoff-panel), `_pollGlobalBatch()` (batch progress bar). Corollary: when JS tracks interactive state flags (e.g., `_cardMenuOpen`) to skip polling re-renders while a dropdown is open, ANY function that replaces the grid via `innerHTML` must also reset the flag: otherwise subsequent polls skip rendering indefinitely until a user click event resets it. This includes `renderTaskCards()` (called from search, expand, collapse), not just the poll handler.
- **New config sections need quadruple registration**: adding a Pydantic model to `ProjectConfig` requires: (1) model class in `sova/config/models.py`, (2) section name in `_NESTED_SECTIONS` in `sova/config/loader.py` (without this, `[section]` in sova.toml is silently ignored), (3) metadata in `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py` for UI display, (4) a matching `_apply_nested_env_overrides()` call inside `_apply_env_overrides()` for every field that should be settable via `SOVA_SECTION_FIELD`. Skipping (4) is easy to miss because (1)-(3) alone make the section work end-to-end for TOML and the dashboard: only the env var path silently no-ops, since `_NESTED_SECTIONS` passes TOML/DB values as init kwargs that Pydantic Settings resolves before env vars are even considered, so nothing raises and nothing looks broken until someone sets the env var and it has no effect. Confirmed twice: `SOVA_FEED_RETENTION_DAYS` (PR #860) and `SOVA_CODEX_MODEL`/`SOVA_CODEX_SANDBOX` (issue #945, PR #1068, CodeRabbit-flagged).
- **Shared single-instance UI state needs its own toggle helpers, not ad-hoc mutation**: the agents page's log viewer is one shared panel (`#log-viewer`) driven by a single `activeLogRunId` global, with a "View Logs" button on every running-agent card and a tab per run inside the panel. Two failure modes came from treating `activeLogRunId = runId` as the whole story: (1) any code path that opens a new run's logs (starting an agent, executing a handoff action, resuming) must only auto-open when `activeLogRunId === null` (unconditionally calling `viewLogs()` steals focus from whatever the user is already watching); centralized in `_autoOpenLogsIfIdle()`. (2) the clicked card's own button label ("View Logs" -> "Viewing Logs") does not repaint itself: `renderAgentCard()` only runs on the next full grid re-render, gated by `agentFingerprint()`, which is driven by an unrelated agent's state change, not by this click. `viewLogs()`/`closeLogViewer()` must each explicitly call `_setLogButtonLabel()` and `updateLogTabs(_lastActiveAgents)` (a cached copy of the last poll's agent list, since the click handler has no fetch of its own) rather than waiting for the poll loop to catch up. `LOG_LEVEL_COLORS` (level -> Tailwind color class) is a shared global in `_head.html`, consumed by both `logs.html` and `agents.html`'s `formatLogLine()` (which parses the `[stderr]/[stdout] <timestamp> [level] event key=value...` structlog `ConsoleRenderer` line format), so the two pages' log level colors cannot drift independently. Files: `sova/dashboard/templates/agents.html`, `sova/dashboard/templates/logs.html`, `sova/dashboard/templates/_head.html`. Issue #973.
- **Per-issue handoff files for parallel agent isolation**: agents write to `handoff-{issue}.json` instead of a shared `handoff.json`. This eliminates race conditions when multiple agents finish concurrently. `write_handoff_file` uses `handoff.issue` for the filename. `read_handoff_file(issue=N)` reads a specific file; without issue, returns the most recent. `read_all_handoff_files` returns all active handoffs. All readers (`_process_auto_handoff`, `_load_review_findings`, `_read_file_handoff`) pass issue context. The `GET /handoff` API returns `{handoff, handoffs[]}` for backward compatibility. Legacy `handoff.json` is supported as a fallback. Files: `sova/ipc/handoff.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/handoff_service.py`.
- **`recover_stale_runs` must check external state for merge-role runs**: when `recover_stale_runs()` finds a dead-PID run with role `integrate-pr` and a `pr_number`, check `_check_pr_merged_on_failure` before marking interrupted. If the PR is merged, mark as "done" instead. Prevents false "interrupted" banners on dashboard restart after the agent completed its work but the process died during cleanup. File: `sova/dashboard/services/agent_recovery.py`.
- **Background merge queue monitor**: `MergeQueueMonitor` dataclass in `sova/dashboard/services/merge_queue_monitor.py` polls `MergeQueueEntry` DB records for PRs enqueued in GitHub merge queues. Runs as background asyncio tasks in dashboard lifespan (one per registered project). Two entry-creation paths: (1) `_check_merge_queue_on_failure()` in `agent_lifecycle.py` when `_wait_and_finalize()` detects a merge-role agent exiting with a PR still in queue, (2) `_check_merge_queue_marker_file()` globs `.claude/agent-control/merge-queue-*.json` (per-PR naming, matching the `handoff-{issue}.json` pattern) written by the `/integrate-pr` command, with legacy `merge-queue.json` fallback. Monitor handles four states: merged (post-merge cleanup: branch delete, issue transition, worktree removal, feed event, notification), ejected (feed event, notification), timeout (configurable via `merge_queue_timeout`), still-queued (log with position). Rate-limit aware via `get_github_quota_tracker()`. NOT_QUEUED fallback checks `_check_pr_merged_directly()` since PRs may merge between polls. Config: `IntegrationConfig` fields `merge_queue_enabled` (str, "true"/"false"), `merge_queue_poll_interval` (int, default 120s), `merge_queue_timeout` (int, default 3600s). Migration 029 adds `merge_queue_entries` table. PR #653.
- **Config-driven provider selection must be wired at startup**: adding a config field (`llm.provider`) + factory (`create_provider()`) + ABC without calling `set_provider(create_provider(cfg.llm.provider))` at app startup means the config has no effect. Wire init in `_init_llm_provider()` (CLI) and `create_app()` (dashboard). For multi-project mode, gate behind `if not is_multi:` or resolve per-request. Files: `sova/llm/provider.py`, `sova/cli/app.py`, `sova/dashboard/app.py`.
- **`pull_request_target` reads workflow from base branch, not PR branch**: GitHub's `pull_request_target` event always uses the workflow YAML from the base branch (e.g., `main`), even for fork PRs. This makes it tamper-proof: fork authors cannot modify the workflow. Use it for security-sensitive automation like auto-approving CI runs or posting status comments. (`sonarcloud.yml` used it for exactly this reason until #1187 moved it to `workflow_run`, which gives the same base-repository context.) The `GITHUB_TOKEN` in `pull_request_target` context has write permissions (unlike `pull_request` for forks, which is read-only). Caveat: CI workflow changes in a PR don't take effect for that PR's own `pull_request_target` run: they only apply after merge. Fork-owner conditions (`github.event.pull_request.author_association`) should be used to scope actions to external contributors. Files: `.github/workflows/fork-pr-gate.yml`.

- **PR spam gate closes drive-by bounty PRs without blocking newcomers**: `PR Quality Gate` (`.github/workflows/pr-quality-gate.yml`) runs on `pull_request_target` for every PR against `main` and evaluates three high-confidence signals in `.github/scripts/detect-pr-spam.sh`: (a) a bounty slash command (`/claim`, `/bounty`, `/reward`) starting a line of the PR body, (b) a changed file whose name carries `bounty`, `claim`, or `reward` as a whole word (so `BOUNTY_FIX.md` matches but `boundary.md` and `reclaim.md` do not), (c) an issue-closing keyword in the body while the diff touches only community meta files (`CODE_OF_CONDUCT.md`, `CONTRIBUTING.md`, `CODEOWNERS`, `LICENSE`) at one of GitHub's own community health file locations (repository root, `.github/`, `docs/`). Rule (c) matches the **full path**, not the basename, so an unrelated nested file such as `templates/CONTRIBUTING.md` never counts as a community file and never puts a genuine PR in scope of the auto-close. `README.md` and ordinary `docs/**` content (anything not named after a community health file) are deliberately excluded from (c) so a genuine typo fix with `Closes #N` is never auto-closed. **The job carries no job-level `if:`**: it is meant to be a required status check, and a job skipped at the job level never reports its check, stranding every PR (the same trap as the docs-only optimization above). The trusted-author filter (`OWNER`, `MEMBER`, `COLLABORATOR`, and `*[bot]` logins) is a step-level early exit, so SOVA's own pipeline PRs are never evaluated and the job still exits 0. Detection lives in a standalone shellcheck-clean bash script rather than inline YAML so every rule is unit-testable (`tests/test_pr_quality_gate.py`); `make lint-bash` and the `Static Checks` shellcheck glob both cover `.github/scripts/*.sh`. Two safety properties are structural: the script **drains stdin before evaluating any rule** (callers pipe the file list in under `set -o pipefail`, so an early exit would kill the producer with SIGPIPE and fail the step on exactly the clearest spam match), and `reason=` is emitted from a fixed token set so a crafted file path can never be echoed into `GITHUB_OUTPUT`. The file-based rules (b) and (c) fail open: an empty changed-file list or a failed API call yields `reason=no-files` and no close. Rule (a) reads only the PR body, is evaluated first, and therefore still applies when the file list is unavailable. No PR head code is checked out, and the PR body reaches the script via the `PR_BODY` env var, never via `${{ }}` interpolation into a run script. `label-gate.yml` posts a one-time deterrent comment when `good first issue` is applied (job-level `if:` is safe there: an issues event is not a required check). Add `PR Quality Gate` to the `main-protection` ruleset only after it has landed on `main` and reported at least one successful run. Issue #818.

- **Pipeline outcome validation detects bypass at exit time**: `_validate_pipeline_outcome()` in `agent_db.py` checks whether a pipeline role (developer, researcher, planner) actually executed WorkflowEngine steps. Primary signal: `current_step == "agent"` (sentinel never cleared) AND 0 `StepExecution` records. Secondary signal (developer-only): `create_pr`/`push` step completed but `pr_number` is None. Tertiary signal (developer-only): `commit` step completed but neither `push` nor `create_pr` ran: pipeline exited mid-flight after committing code. Quaternary signal (all pipeline roles): last `StepExecution` has `status=interrupted` (set by `_finalize_orphaned_steps` when the process exits with a step still running): the pipeline crashed or exited silently mid-step. Called from `_wait_and_finalize()` after `_validate_command_outcome()` (they are mutually exclusive: command roles vs pipeline roles). Downgrades "done" to "failed" via `_downgrade_to_failed()`. `_PIPELINE_ROLES = {"developer", "researcher", "planner"}` excludes `reviewer` (it's in `_STANDALONE_ROLES` and doesn't use WorkflowEngine). Files: `sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_lifecycle.py`. Related issues: #343 (auto-retry), #344 (runtime watchdog), #628 (orphaned steps).
- **Orphaned step finalization on terminal transition**: `_finalize_orphaned_steps()` in `agent_db.py` runs inside `_finalize_task_run()` after setting the terminal status. Updates any `StepExecution` records still in `status=running` to `status=interrupted` with `ended_at=now()`. Without this, the dashboard step progress bar shows "running" for a step in a terminal run. Called within the same DB transaction as the TaskRun status update for consistency.
- **Auto-retry for recoverable pipeline failures: REMOVED.** The `_schedule_retry()` / `classify_failure()` / `_RECOVERABLE_PATTERNS` / `retry_of_id` / `retry_count` system described in earlier versions of this doc was dropped in migration `020_drop_task_run_retry_columns.py` (2026-07-20); none of those names exist in `sova/` anymore. When a pipeline role fails, the user must restart it manually from the dashboard. Re-implementation is tracked under issues #343 (auto-retry) and #344 (runtime watchdog); the prior design is preserved for reference in `.claude/agent-memory/cookbook.md`.

- **`budget_override` is distinct from `force`**: `force` means "skip pipeline gate checks" for many unrelated reasons (CLI `--force`, `resume_from_approval()`, retry spawns). The per-issue budget guard (`start_agent()`'s pre-check in `agent_lifecycle.py`, `WorkflowEngine._check_per_issue_budget()` in `workflow.py`) is gated on a separate `budget_override` flag (`ExecutionContext.budget_override`, CLI `--budget-override`, dashboard `StartAgentRequest.budget_override`) so a caller passing `force=True` for an unrelated reason does not silently bypass the budget check and pollute the `BudgetOverride` audit table with unconfirmed overrides. Only the dashboard's confirmation modal (`agents.html`) and an explicit CLI flag set `budget_override=True`; `resume_from_approval()` intentionally passes only `force=True` and still gets the hard budget stop if the issue is over budget.
- **GitHub API calls must not run inside DB write transactions in sweep/recovery paths**: `_liveness_sweep_once` and `recover_stale_runs` use a three-phase pattern to avoid write lock contention: (1) read-only query with no write lock, (2) I/O checks (GitHub API) outside any session with bounded timeout, (3) write-only transaction for status updates. In the write phase, guard with `if run.status in _TERMINAL: continue`: `_wait_and_finalize()` may have finalized the run between phases 1 and 3. For `recover_stale_runs`, use `asyncio.gather` for concurrent checks wrapped in outer `asyncio.wait_for(timeout=20)` so N stale merge-role runs don't multiply startup time. Files: `sova/dashboard/app.py:_liveness_sweep_once`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_recovery.py`. PR #364.

- **`_resolve_issue_worktree` creates worktrees proactively**: when a branch is known but no worktree directory exists (common for issue-less PRs), calls `create_worktree()`: naming: `pr-{N}` for issue-less PRs, `{issue_id}` for issue-linked PRs, sanitized branch name otherwise. Falls back to `project_dir` only on git failure. Both `start_agent` and `start_command` thread `pr_number` through to this function. Prevents agents from running in the main repo and triggering uvicorn `--reload` via watched file changes in `sova/dashboard/`. File: `sova/dashboard/services/agent_context.py:_resolve_issue_worktree`. PR #364.
