Two additional layers were added alongside, independent of this guard: `TrustedHostMiddleware` (`allowed_hosts` from `build_allowed_hosts()`, the same loopback-aware host set as `build_allowed_origins()` minus scheme/port, pinned to `["*"]` for a wildcard bind since there is no single derivable host; `"test"`/`"testserver"` are permanently included to match httpx's `ASGITransport`/Starlette's `TestClient` default `Host` headers, a concession that costs nothing since neither is a registrable production hostname and a spoofed one still cannot pass the separate Origin/CSRF check, which never allowlists it), and a `security_headers` middleware setting `Content-Security-Policy`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`, `X-Frame-Options: DENY`, and `Referrer-Policy: strict-origin-when-cross-origin` on every response (the CSP keeps `'unsafe-inline'` for scripts/styles, since templates use inline `<script>` blocks and `onclick` handlers extensively and a nonce/hash rewrite is template-by-template work out of this issue's scope). The one A2A mutation (`POST /a2a/tasks/{task_id}/cancel`), previously unauthenticated, now requires a bearer token validated by `mcp_service.validate_mcp_token()` against the exact `run_id` the task ID encodes, reusing the MCP HMAC scheme rather than inventing a new one, via a dependency (`_validate_a2a_cancel_auth`) that also runs `_require_a2a_enabled()` first so a disabled A2A config still answers 404 ahead of any auth check. The MCP JSON-RPC endpoint itself was tightened too: `_filter_tool_arguments()` now drops any client-supplied argument a tool's own `inspect.signature()` does not declare, and unconditionally drops `project_dir` even when declared, since every `mcp_service` tool scopes its DB session by that parameter and forwarding a client value verbatim would let a token valid for one run redirect a query at an arbitrary other project. `POST /api/agents/command`'s `command` field gained the same treatment: `known_command_names()` (`sova/dashboard/services/agent_context.py`) mirrors `_resolve_command_prompt()`'s own directory lookup order and its exact match criterion, file existence by filename stem, and the router now rejects (400) a name outside that set, closing both the path-traversal and prompt-injection angle the previous bare fallback (`f"/{command}"` sent straight to the headless agent) left open. Deliberately globs `*.md` by stem rather than calling `sova.commands.catalog.discover()`: `discover()` additionally filters to files with a valid frontmatter `name:` field, a stricter criterion than `_resolve_command_prompt()`'s, so it would have 400'd two real, resolvable commands that happen to lack that field (`agent-resume.md`, `request-review.md`). Files: `sova/dashboard/security.py`, `sova/dashboard/app.py`, `sova/dashboard/static/app.js`, `sova/dashboard/routers/agents.py`, `sova/dashboard/routers/a2a.py`, `sova/dashboard/routers/mcp.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_context.py`. Issue #1204.
- **A read-only provider status widget must not lift the availability check's fail-open contract into its own "authenticated" answer**: `GET /api/auth/status` (`sova/dashboard/routers/auth.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/setup_service.py:get_auth_status()`) is deliberately read-only, no server-driven OAuth flow, since the dashboard cannot safely hold or refresh a user's `claude` CLI session on their behalf; it reports the configured provider, whether it authenticates, and (for providers exposing `get_auth_details()`) the account identity behind it, so the widget can render a copy-paste recovery command (`claude auth login`/`claude setup-token`) instead. Follows the single-user loopback deployment model documented in the entry above: no per-request auth dependency, matching every other read-only dashboard GET endpoint (costs, runs, logs, memory); CodeRabbit raised this as a CWE-200 finding during review and withdrew it after verifying the endpoint returns only allowlisted, non-secret fields and that real per-endpoint auth is out of scope for #931 (which covers credential-mutating routes, not status reads). Cached per project directory for `_AUTH_STATUS_CACHE_TTL` seconds behind a per-key `asyncio.Lock` single-flight guard (mirroring `coderabbit_quota.py`'s pattern) so concurrent pollers share one CLI probe. Because `ClaudeCodeProvider.check_available()` intentionally fails open (reports available with `"auth state unknown"`) when the CLI build lacks the `auth` subcommand or returns unparseable output, `get_auth_status()` cannot use that return value directly as the widget's `authenticated` field: doing so reported a confirmed-looking green status for a state that was never confirmed. Providers exposing `get_auth_details()` have `authenticated` overridden to `account is not None` instead; providers without it (no confirmed/unconfirmed distinction available) keep the raw `check_available()` result. File: `sova/dashboard/services/setup_service.py`, `sova/dashboard/routers/auth.py`, `sova/dashboard/static/provider-status-widget.js`. Issue #933.
- **A convention that permits N near-duplicate implementations still needs a shared helper once SonarCloud's 3% new-code duplication gate is in scope**: this repo's own stated convention (documented in code comments and PR descriptions) is that two occurrences of a duplicated block are fine and only 3+ warrants extraction. That local convention is about readability, not about the SonarCloud Quality Gate, which measures duplicated *lines* against a fixed 3% threshold on new code regardless of occurrence count: two nearly-identical ~30-line command functions can trip it well before a third copy exists. First seen in PR #334 (`resume_from_approval` / `reject_spec` sharing CAS validation logic), confirmed again in PR #1024 (`sync_cmd` / `skills_sync_cmd` in `sova/cli/commands/commands.py`, both looping `list_projects()` and reporting per-project/summary status, differing only in which `update_*()` function and subdirectory they target). Fix: extract the shared body into a helper parameterized by the one or two things that actually vary (a target status, or a `Callable[[Path, ProjectConfig], UpdateResult]` plus a subdirectory) rather than waiting for a third occurrence. Verify locally before pushing: `sonar-scanner` isn't required, but a rough gut-check is `git diff --stat` on the two near-duplicate functions relative to their combined size, and the gate only measures the diff's *added* lines, so refactoring pre-existing code to share a helper removes the duplication from the count entirely.
- **Planner prompt sections are row-capped, not character-budgeted, and the health widget is in-memory only**: `SupervisorPlanner._get_issue_health()` and `_get_recent_failures()` (`sova/supervisor/planner.py`) cap the queue rows and failure rows they render, via `supervisor.planner_issue_health_max_rows` (default 50) and `supervisor.planner_max_failures_in_context` (default 20), so prompt size (and therefore p90 latency) no longer scales with queue length the way it did before these caps existed. A truncated section always appends a trailing `_Showing N of M ..._` line so the model is never misled into treating a partial view as exhaustive; `_get_recent_failures()` gets the true total `M` from a separate `COUNT(*)` query rather than comparing `len(rows) == cap`, because a `.limit(cap)` query can never return more than `cap` rows, so that comparison is a false positive whenever the true count happens to equal the cap exactly. `_call_llm()` retries only `LLMTimeoutError` (`supervisor.planner_max_attempts`, default 3, exponential backoff seeded by `supervisor.planner_retry_backoff_seconds`, default 1.0, doubling per attempt; `0` is a valid, supported value that retries immediately with no sleep, so the field is `ge=0` not `gt=0`) since a retry cannot fix an exhausted budget, a missing model, or malformed JSON; those exceptions and JSON parse failures return `None` immediately and fall back to deterministic mode on the same poll cycle (backoff is never carried across poll cycles). Success/failure outcomes feed a dashboard "Planner Health" widget (`GET /api/supervisor/planner-health`) that is **in-memory only**, not a `SupervisorDecision` DB row: `record_planner_outcome()`/`get_planner_health()` in `sova/dashboard/services/supervisor_service.py` maintain a `_planner_health` dict keyed by project slug, mirroring the existing ephemeral `_pending_plan` pattern, because a diagnostic success/failure tally does not warrant permanent storage and unbounded per-cycle DB growth. The router resolves the same `resolve_project_slug(cfg.github_repo, project_dir)` key the planner itself writes to (matching the `/plan` endpoint's pattern), not the looser `cfg.github_repo or None` used by `/decisions` and `/counts`. Since the widget resets on every server restart, `supervisor.html`'s `loadPlannerHealth()` fires one `POST /api/supervisor/poll` (once per page load, guarded by `_plannerHealthAutoPolled`) when it sees an all-zero result, so a first visit after a restart doesn't sit empty until the next `poll_interval_seconds` elapses. Field names here (`planner_max_attempts`, `planner_retry_backoff_seconds`, `planner_issue_health_max_rows`, `planner_max_failures_in_context`) diverge from an earlier spec draft that proposed `planner_enabled`/`planner_max_retries`/`planner_retry_timeout_multiplier`/`planner_health_retention_cycles`: `supervisor.llm_planning` already toggles the planner on/off (a separate `planner_enabled` would duplicate it), the implemented backoff is a fixed base delay that doubles per attempt rather than a growing per-attempt timeout multiplier, and health-count retention/decay across cycles was dropped as unnecessary now that the widget is in-memory and resets on restart rather than accumulating forever in the DB. Files: `sova/supervisor/planner.py`, `sova/config/models.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/supervisor_service.py`, `sova/dashboard/routers/supervisor.py`, `sova/dashboard/templates/supervisor.html`, `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`. Issue #937.

- **`.claude/commands/` is a rendered build artifact, not a hand-maintained tree**: this repo is both the canonical source of the distributable slash commands (`sova/commands/data/commands/*.md`, carrying `{{ var }}` placeholders) and a sync target for them (`.claude/commands/`, the copy Claude Code actually loads). Claude Code has no templating, so an unrendered `{{ check_cmd }}` reaches the agent verbatim: seven commands (`develop`, `develop-full`, `pr`, `review`, `review-full`, `simplify`, `test`) shipped a literal placeholder instead of `make check` until #1088. Because the rendered tree was hand-maintained, it also drifted from canonical in both directions: `pr.md` (+59 lines), `health-audit.md` (+156), `ingest-review.md` (+52) and `extract-knowledge.md` (+11) were richer on the project side and those improvements never reached the other registered projects, while `optimize-knowledge.md` was a symlink back into `sova/commands/data/commands/` (a hazard, since `_install_files()` writes through a symlink and would overwrite the template it rendered from). The manifest cannot distinguish a deliberate project-specific variant from stale installed content, so `sova commands sync` overwrote the drift and left the primary checkout dirty; `ensure_claude_artifacts()` then mirrored that dirt into every worktree, where `RearrangeCommitsStep.validate_output()` counted it as the agent's own uncommitted work and paused the run (17 gate failures, $32.65 of unshipped address-review work, and PR #1086 stuck four rounds behind its own fixes). `sova/commands/self_render.py:render_self()` is now the single regeneration path, shared by `make commands-render` and the drift guard in `tests/test_command_render_drift.py`, and it delegates to `install_commands()` so regeneration exercises the same code path a real `sova install` takes. Its variable values are pinned in `SELF_VARIABLES` rather than read from `load_config()`, because a checked-in artifact must be reproducible from the repository alone: `load_config()` resolves `check_cmd` from `.claude/sova.db`, which does not exist in CI, so the same template would render as `make check` locally and as the `lint_cmd && test_cmd` fallback on a clean checkout. The guard fails with an instruction to run `make commands-render` rather than regenerating anything itself, matching the marketplace precedent below; `test_every_used_placeholder_is_pinned` additionally fails when canonical gains a placeholder with no pinned value, since `render_command()` leaves an unknown placeholder as-is by design. The eight project-only commands (`agent-resume`, `audit-alignment`, `benchmark-report`, `design`, `quarterly-report`, `request-review`, `sprint-plan`, `verify-local`) stay outside the manifest and are never touched. Files: `sova/commands/self_render.py`, `tests/test_command_render_drift.py`, `Makefile`. Issue #1088.

- **Marketplace plugin is generated, never hand-authored**: `plugins/sova/` packages a subset of `sova/commands/data/commands/` (`develop`, `spec`, `review`, `pr`, `debug`, `test`, the self-contained workflow commands that need no SOVA infrastructure) as a standalone Claude Code plugin for the [AI Helpers Marketplace](https://github.com/openshift-eng/ai-helpers). Early versions hand-authored the six `plugins/sova/commands/*.md` files independently of `sova/commands/data/commands/`, which let them silently diverge from the canonical source they were meant to package. `sova/commands/marketplace_export.py` (`make marketplace`) fixes this by mechanically deriving the marketplace package: `render_command(name)` reads a canonical `sova/commands/data/commands/{name}.md`, applies text substitutions (SOVA `{{ template_vars }}` and `sova.toml` references become generic project-agnostic wording, ` -- ` prose separators become `: ` per the `no-double-dash` invariant, skipping fenced code), and reshapes the frontmatter and body into the marketplace's `## Name`/`## Synopsis`/`## Description`/`## Implementation` section format. `plugin.json`'s version is read from `pyproject.toml` (`read_pyproject_version()`) rather than hand-set, so it cannot drift from the package's actual version; `_sync_marketplace_json()` then copies `plugin.json`'s `name`/`description`/`version` into the root `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` entry, making `plugin.json` the single source of truth for both files. `export()` also writes `.marketplace-manifest.json` (source file SHA-256 hashes) so `tests/test_marketplace_plugin.py::TestNoDrift` can catch drift two ways: `test_generated_command_matches_source` re-renders each command and asserts byte-equality with the checked-in file, and `test_manifest_source_hashes_match_current_commands` asserts no canonical source changed since the last export. Both fail with an instruction to run `make marketplace` and commit the result, rather than fixing anything themselves: the marketplace package is a build artifact, and CI must not silently regenerate it. `Illustrative example invocations (_EXAMPLES)` remain the one piece of per-command metadata authored by hand, since a realistic example argument cannot be mechanically derived from a command's frontmatter. Files: `sova/commands/marketplace_export.py`, `sova/commands/catalog.py` (`parse_frontmatter()` extracted for reuse), `tests/test_marketplace_plugin.py`, `Makefile`. Issue #324.
- **State label swaps must replace the full label array in one call, not clear-then-add**: `gh issue edit --add-label X --remove-label Y` looks atomic but issues two separate GraphQL mutations (`removeLabelsFromLabelable` then `addLabelsToLabelable`) even from one CLI invocation, leaving a window where a concurrent reader (another role's gate check, a second `transition_state()` call racing the first) observes zero `agent:*` state labels and falls back to `TaskState.BACKLOG`, or where two interleaved calls each clear only the label they saw and leave two conflicting state labels stuck. `GitHubAdapter._set_state_label()` fixes this by reading the issue's current labels once, computing the full replacement array (existing non-state labels plus the new state label, with every `_STATE_LABELS` value stripped), and sending it as a single `gh api repos/{repo}/issues/{n} --method PATCH --input -` call, which GitHub's REST API applies as one atomic label-set replacement. `_patch_labels()` retains the auto-create-on-missing-label retry (`gh label create` then re-PATCH) that `_add_label()` already had, scoped only to the new state label since every other label in the payload was already confirmed to exist by virtue of being read back from the issue. `JiraAdapter._set_state_label()` closes the same gap differently: Jira Cloud's REST v3 issue-update API accepts mixed `{"remove": ...}`/`{"add": ...}` operations against the same field in one `update.labels` array, so the stale-label removals and the new label addition are sent as a single `PUT`, instead of the previous `_clear_state_labels()`-then-`add_label()` two-call sequence. Never reach for `gh issue edit --add-label/--remove-label` for state transitions; it is not atomic despite looking like a single command. **A single atomic write per call does not make two concurrent calls to the same issue safe**: neither GitHub's nor Jira's REST APIs support conditional/If-Match requests on these endpoints, so a genuinely concurrent pair of calls each computes its payload from the same stale read. For GitHub's full-array PATCH this can silently drop a non-state label added by a different concurrent writer (e.g. `_add_label()` adding `role:*`) between the read and the PATCH; for Jira's relative add/remove ops it cannot drop unrelated labels (the ops are deltas, not a full replacement) but two concurrent `_set_state_label()` calls can each remove the same stale label and add a different new one, leaving both new state labels stuck. Both adapters close this for calls made through the same adapter instance with a per-issue `asyncio.Lock` (`GitHubAdapter._label_lock()`, shared by `_set_state_label()`, `_add_label()`, and `remove_label()`, since a removal completing inside the read-then-PATCH window is otherwise reversed when the PATCH writes back the label from its now-stale `final_labels` snapshot; `JiraAdapter._label_lock()`, used by `_set_state_label()` only, since Jira's `add_label()`/`remove_label()` are delta ops and not vulnerable to the drop). Verified empirically in both adapters' test suites by forcing a real interleaving window (an `asyncio.Event` barrier for GitHub's mocked `gh` calls; a `respx` async side effect with `asyncio.sleep()` for Jira's real httpx transport) and confirming the race reproduces with the lock removed and disappears with it present. Neither lock protects against another process, another SOVA instance, or a human editing labels in the GitHub/Jira UI concurrently: that would require server-side conditional writes that these APIs do not offer. Files: `sova/adapters/github.py`, `sova/adapters/jira.py`, `tests/test_adapters.py`, `tests/test_jira_adapter.py`. Issue #970.
- **A stale `already_running` gate result must not permanently starve an issue's later gates**: `check_already_running()`'s query already excludes every terminal `TaskRun.status` (`_ALREADY_RUNNING_TERMINAL = TASK_RUN_TERMINAL - {"awaiting_approval"}`), and a real-DB regression test (`TestAlreadyRunningReadConsistency` in `tests/test_progression.py`: finalize a run to `status="failed"` via one file-backed session, re-check via a separately-opened session from the same factory) passes against current code, ruling out session/connection-level read staleness as a reproducible bug: the read side was never the fault. What the deterministic engine already guarantees, and what was previously undocumented, is that this cannot wedge an issue permanently: `evaluate_all()` re-evaluates every task in the resolved queue every poll cycle unconditionally (`_determine_transition()` maps `TaskState.IN_PROGRESS` to `RESET_STALE_STATE` on every call, with no per-issue skip-list or cross-cycle memory), so a stale `already_running` false positive on one poll self-heals the moment the gate reports the agent gone on the next poll: `TestAutoRebase.test_stale_already_running_wait_self_heals_to_spawn_rebase` locks this in by calling `_evaluate_single()` twice for the same issue (`IN_PROGRESS` + stale block -> `WAIT`, then `IN_REVIEW` + conflict-only blocker -> `SPAWN_REBASE`) and asserting the second call is unaffected by the first. The one gap self-heal cannot cover is an issue that was in scope for this cycle's scan but silently drops out of it (e.g. a mid-loop rate-limit break or a graph-cache gap), since it then never gets a fresh decision to self-correct from. `TaskProgressionEngine._check_decision_staleness()` (called at the end of `evaluate_all()`) is the watchdog for that gap: for every non-`DONE` entry in the cycle's resolved `task_ids` (not `graph.nodes`) absent from `evaluated_issues`, it looks up the issue's most recent `SupervisorDecision` (`component="progression"`) and, if the gap exceeds `supervisor.decision_staleness_threshold_seconds` (default 1800s), logs `evaluate_all.decision_stale` and emits a warning feed event; an issue with no prior decision, or one that is legitimately `DONE`, is never flagged. Comparing against `task_ids` rather than every graph node matters: `task_queue` is an intentionally exclusive filter (capacity-limited by `max_queue_size`, pruned by queue maintenance, deprioritized by the planner), so an open backlog issue that simply isn't queued this cycle is working as designed, not stalled: comparing against the whole graph would flag the entire backlog beyond the queue as "dropped out" on every single cycle forever. Fails open (returns `[]`) on any DB error, and does not alter, block, or filter the returned `decisions` list: purely diagnostic. Config field triple-registered (`sova/config/models.py`, `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`; no `_NESTED_SECTIONS` entry needed since it lives inside the existing `[supervisor]` section). Files: `sova/supervisor/progression.py`, `sova/supervisor/gates/already_running.py`, `sova/config/models.py`, `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`, `tests/test_progression.py`. Issue #984.
- **A stale `RESET_STALE_STATE` candidate must not roll back an issue that already has an open PR**: `_evaluate_single()`'s `RESET_STALE_STATE` branch previously had only one guard, `check_already_running()`. An agent that dies (or a dashboard reload that drops `_wait_and_finalize`) anywhere in the window between `create_pr` and `handoff_to_reviewer` in the 17-step developer pipeline leaves the issue labeled `in_progress` with a healthy open PR already attached; the next poll cycle sees no live agent and rolled it back via `_execute_stale_reset()` regardless, so a subsequent `SPAWN_DEVELOPER` cycle developed the same issue again, producing a second PR for one issue. `ProgressionAction.REPAIR_TO_IN_REVIEW` closes this: once `check_already_running()` passes (no live agent), and only when `self._adapter.repo` is set, `_evaluate_single()` calls `find_pr_for_issue_checked()` (`sova/git/pr.py`); an open PR (draft included, since `--state open` covers drafts) returns `REPAIR_TO_IN_REVIEW` instead of `RESET_STALE_STATE`, and `_execute_repair_to_in_review()` transitions the issue forward to `IN_REVIEW` via `self._adapter.transition_state()` rather than rolling it back, so the next cycle picks it up through the existing `_refine_in_review_action()` path. A non-GitHub adapter (`self._adapter.repo` falsy, e.g. Jira) skips the check entirely and falls through to today's reset behavior unchanged. `find_pr_for_issue_checked()` is a `raise_on_failure=True` sibling of `find_pr_for_issue()`, sharing the same 90-second TTL cache (`_find_pr_for_issue_cached()`, the shared cache/lookup path both wrap): a lookup failure (shell/API error, or a GitHub-quota-forced skip of the branch-name fallback search) raises `PRLookupError` instead of silently returning `None`, since `find_pr_for_issue()`'s existing contract makes "no PR found" and "couldn't confirm" indistinguishable, and treating the latter as the former would incorrectly reset an issue whose PR status is simply unknown. Only a confirmed result (found, or confirmed-empty because at least one of the body/branch searches actually completed) is cached; a failed lookup is never cached, so the next call re-checks rather than pinning a 90-second false negative. `REPAIR_TO_IN_REVIEW` is actionable (not in `NON_ACTIONABLE_ACTIONS`) but excluded from slot/quota decrement in `_update_remaining_capacity()`, mirroring `RESET_STALE_STATE`: like that action, it is subject to `supervisor.require_approval`'s pending-plan gate and to LLM-plan filtering when `llm_planning` is enabled, the same as the reset path it replaces. Files: `sova/supervisor/progression.py`, `sova/git/pr.py`, `tests/test_progression.py`, `tests/test_git_pr_rate_limit.py`. Issue #994 (part of epic #986).

- **The review loop must close without a human click, and every resolver action must be a supervisor action**: after #1062 defaulted the `sova_reviewed` integration gate on, an address cycle left every PR at "Review Addressed" with Integrate disabled ("a re-review must approve the new head") and the only way forward was a manual Review click, then a manual Integrate. Four gaps combined. (1) `resolve_next_action()` evaluated the addressed and no-review routes to `review_pr` before the external-changes rule, so a bot CHANGES_REQUESTED posted after the address round (PR #1070: two unresolved Major threads) was hidden behind a Review button; the external rule now precedes both (golden rows `addressed_but_external_changes_requested_routes_to_address_pr` and friends in `tests/test_work_item_service.py`). (2) `_ACTION_ID_TO_PROGRESSION` mapped only `integrate`/`address_review`/`rebase`, so `review_pr` and `address_pr` fell to `CHECKPOINT_NEEDED` and the supervisor could never re-review, address external findings, or act on a CI-failed PR on its own; `SPAWN_REVIEWER` (role `reviewer`, gated by the new `supervisor.auto_review`, default off like its siblings) and `SPAWN_ADDRESS_PR` (role `command:address-pr`, gated by the existing `auto_address_review`) are now real actions, both PR-scoped (`_PR_SCOPED_ACTIONS`) and the latter bounded by the same circuit breaker as the pipeline path (`_ADDRESS_CYCLE_ACTIONS`; both the gate and this set were later deleted by #1110, which moved the cap into `resolve_next_action()` itself, see "Address-review budget is a resolver state" below). (3) The address-review pipeline ended in `HandoffToUserStep` with `next_action=integrate` while the gate demanded a re-review, so the pipeline's own handoff and the gate contradicted each other; it now ends in `HandoffToReviewerStep` (phase `address_review`, auto per `pipeline.auto_handoff`), and the stale-review guard in `_process_auto_handoff()` treats an `addressed` verdict as "re-review the new head" instead of suppressing the spawn as a duplicate (`test_auto_handoff_spawns_rereview_when_verdict_is_addressed`). The re-review costs about $0.65 per reviewer run against $2.5 to $11.8 per `/address-pr` run, so chaining it is preferred over weakening the gate to accept "addressed". (4) a command-backed action never reached its command: `_ACTION_TO_ROLE` mapped `SPAWN_INTEGRATE` to the role string `command:integrate-pr` and `execute_decision()` passed it to `start_agent()`, which forwards its role to `sova run --role`, where `dispatch()` resolves it through `get_role_async()` and raises `Unknown role: 'command:integrate-pr'` (only `developer`, `planner`, `researcher`, `reviewer`, `triage` and DB-backed custom roles exist). The spawned Claude agent was left to improvise the whole workflow from a failed CLI call, which is why supervisor-driven integrate runs succeeded at all. `_ACTION_TO_COMMAND` now routes both command-backed actions through `start_command()`, which sends the real slash command and records the run under the same `command:{name}` role string that the address-cycle queries match on; `test_command_actions_never_reach_start_agent` is the regression guard. This mattered doubly for the new `SPAWN_ADDRESS_PR`, since an improvised `/address-pr` rewrites branch history. (5) `count_address_review_runs()` (`sova/supervisor/gates/utils.py`) counted only pipeline runs, so the three `command:address-pr` runs on PR #1070 that each re-triggered `@coderabbitai review` and got a fresh CHANGES_REQUESTED back were invisible to `max_address_review_cycles`; it now counts both shapes for the PR, including runs whose `issue_number` is NULL (a command run started before the PR body linked its issue, #1066) while still excluding runs recorded against a different issue, mirroring `_address_cycle_completed_since()`, and `/address-pr` step 17 skips the bot re-review trigger once `ROUND` exceeds the cap (read from `project_settings` in the primary checkout, since the command runs in a worktree). Step 15 computes `ROUND` from the `sova-addressed` markers already on the PR instead of asking the model to count (a fourth round had been labelled "Round 1"). The addressed-findings prompt section the Reviewer receives (`_format_addressed_findings()`) now also renders SOVA review findings (`file`/`description` keys, grouped under `sova-review`), not only external-tool findings. Files: `sova/dashboard/services/work_state.py`, `sova/supervisor/progression.py`, `sova/supervisor/gates/utils.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_progress.py`, `sova/core/steps/__init__.py`, `sova/core/steps/handoff_to_reviewer.py`, `sova/core/context.py`, `sova/roles/developer.py`, `sova/roles/_review_comments.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_handoff.py`, `sova/config/models.py`, `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`, `sova/commands/data/commands/address-pr.md` (mirrored in `.claude/commands/`).
- **Address-review budget is a resolver state, not a separate gate**: an over-budget PR used to be caught by two independent mechanisms, a supervisor gate (`sova/supervisor/gates/circuit_breaker.py`) and the auto-handoff breaker in `agent_handoff.py`, each writing its own manual-only handoff with a slightly different message. `resolve_next_action()` (`sova/dashboard/services/work_state.py`) now owns the cap directly: its `review_budget_exhausted` rule compares `PRFacts.address_cycles` (from `resolve_sova_verdict()`, which at the time derived it via `count_address_review_runs()`, a DB query since replaced by counting `sova-addressed` GitHub review markers directly, issue #1112) against `PRFacts.max_address_cycles` (`pipeline.max_address_review_cycles`, default 3, 0=unlimited) and resolves an over-budget PR to `WorkItemState.PR_REVIEW_EXHAUSTED` with `action_id="integrate"`, ahead of every verdict- and thread-based rule so the loop cannot spawn one more cycle regardless of a standing revise/block verdict. Because that is the same action id `PR_READY_TO_MERGE` emits, the supervisor needs no dedicated branch or flag: it flows through the existing `_ACTION_ID_TO_PROGRESSION["integrate"]` mapping under `supervisor.auto_integrate`, and `sova/supervisor/gates/circuit_breaker.py` plus the `_ADDRESS_CYCLE_ACTIONS` gate block in `progression.py` were deleted as a result. **It does need the cap threaded, though, and that is now the only thing bounding it**: `max_address_cycles` is a keyword parameter defaulting to 0 (unlimited), so `_refine_in_review_action()` passing `self._config.pipeline.max_address_review_cycles` into `_build_pr_facts()` is load-bearing. Omitting it leaves the supervisor reading its own budget as unlimited while the gate that used to catch that case no longer exists, which re-opens the infinite address-review loop for the autonomous path only, invisibly, since the dashboard (which threads the cap from `get_work_items()`) still renders the exhausted state correctly. `tests/test_progression.py::TestRefineInReviewAction::test_exhausted_address_budget_never_spawns_another_address_cycle` is the regression guard. The count itself reaches the facts via the verdict dict, so `_merge_label_verdict()` re-attaches `address_cycles` from the DB-derived verdict onto a label-sourced one before returning it: `_extract_sova_verdict_from_labels()` carries no cycle count, so a label win would otherwise reset the budget to 0 and silently disable the rule for exactly the cross-instance PRs most likely to be over it. `_attach_integration_gates()` skips gate evaluation entirely for a `PR_REVIEW_EXHAUSTED` item so the Integrate button renders enabled even though the standing verdict would otherwise fail the `sova_reviewed` gate: once the budget is exhausted there is no more autonomous fixing left to re-review, and the whole point of this state is to hand the decision to a human. `_check_address_review_circuit_breaker()` still runs at agent-exit time (the resolver only re-evaluates on the next poll, so something still has to stop the handoff chain from auto-spawning one more cycle the instant the budget-exhausting agent exits), but its output shrank to clearing the stale handoff plus one `warning`-severity feed event plus one best-effort notification through the shared `notify()` fan-out (not `send_desktop_notification()` directly, so the operator's `[notification]` config is honoured and delivery stays fire-and-forget on a path that must not block on a notifier subprocess); it invalidates the per-PR verdict cache (`invalidate_verdict()`) unconditionally, once the cycle count is computed rather than only when it turns out to be exhausted, so a verdict served from cache always reflects the just-completed cycle instead of waiting out the cache TTL. **That invalidation call is gated on `role == "developer"`, which only covers one of the two run shapes `count_address_review_runs()` counts.** A `command:address-pr` run (the `/address-pr` command path) also increments the real cycle count but never passes through `_check_address_review_circuit_breaker()`, so a verdict cached before that run completed kept serving its stale `address_cycles` for up to the full 5-minute positive TTL, during which `review_budget_exhausted` could compare against a count one cycle behind and let the supervisor spawn one past the cap (self-review finding on PR #1133, since `_process_auto_handoff()` only acts on an existing auto-executable handoff and the command path writes none). Fixed at the read side instead of adding a second gated invalidation call site: `resolve_sova_verdict()` now recomputes `address_cycles` via `_safe_count_address_cycles()` on every call, cache hit or miss, rather than trusting whatever count a hit's cache entry was written with. This is deliberately more robust than enumerating every completion path that can increment the count, and cheap, since `count_address_review_runs()` is a local DB read with no GitHub API call, far below the cost the verdict cache exists to avoid. **This whole DB-counting/recompute-on-every-call mechanism was later deleted (issue #1112)**: `resolve_sova_verdict()` now derives `address_cycles` from `parse_review_history()`'s count of `sova-addressed` GitHub review markers, computed from the same fetch that produces the verdict itself, so the stale-cache problem this paragraph solves no longer exists (there is nothing left to recompute separately; see "GitHub review markers are the sole SOVA verdict source" above). **That same GitHub-markers rewrite introduced a distinct unresolvable case the old DB count never had**: `resolve_sova_verdict()` returns `work_verdict.py`'s `UNRESOLVED_KEY`-tagged verdict when GitHub itself could not be reached, and `address_cycles` on that verdict defaults to 0 (indistinguishable from "no review yet" in the raw dict). `_check_address_review_circuit_breaker()` already failed closed on this flag, but `_build_pr_facts()`/`resolve_next_action()`, the one resolver both the dashboard and supervisor share, did not: it read the 0 at face value and could authorize one more cycle during exactly the GitHub outage the flag exists to flag (self-review finding, PR #1145). Fixed by adding `PRFacts.review_history_unresolved` (set in `_build_pr_facts()` from the same key) and folding it into the `review_budget_exhausted` rule alongside the cycle-count check, both gated on `max_address_cycles > 0` so an unlimited budget still has nothing to fail closed on. Files: `sova/dashboard/services/work_state.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/work_verdict.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/work_item_service.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_handoff.py`, `sova/supervisor/progression.py`, `sova/config/models.py`. Issue #1110, #1112.

- **A review is only trackable if each finding gets its own thread, and both review paths must produce one**: SOVA has two review implementations. `ReviewerRole._post_review()` (role `reviewer`, used by the developer handoff chain and by the supervisor's `SPAWN_REVIEWER`) posts a formal review whose findings become inline comments via `_build_review_comments()` plus `parse_diff_lines()`, so every finding that lands on a line present on the RIGHT side of the diff opens its own resolvable thread. The `/review-pr` command (role `command:review-pr`, what the dashboard's Review button runs) posted only a summary body: `gh api .../reviews -f event -f body`, with no `comments[]` array. Same command file on every project, same LLM findings, completely different trackability, and the divergence was invisible because both produce an identically formatted body through `format_from_json()`. Consequences: a command-driven review left nothing per-finding to resolve, so the unresolved-thread count (which drives `thread_signal`, the `threads_resolved` integration gate, and the at-a-glance "what is still open" reading of the agents page) reflected only external bots; and an autonomous re-review of the same PR produced richer output than a human clicking Review on it. `build_review_payload_from_json(json_text, diff_text, event)` in `sova/roles/_review_comments.py` closes it by building the complete `{body, event, comments}` payload from the command's own findings JSON plus the PR diff, reusing the role's `_build_review_comments()` so the two paths cannot drift again; `_finding_from_dict()` is defensive about the LLM-authored JSON (a bool, a non-numeric string or a missing `line` degrades to a body-only finding instead of raising, and bool is rejected explicitly since it is a subclass of int). The command saves the diff it already fetches in step 1 and posts with `--input`, with three ordered fallbacks mirroring the role and the adapter: self-review 422 rebuilds with `event=COMMENT`, a rejected inline position retries with `comments: []`, and an unimportable SOVA (or a missing diff file) falls back to the body-only form. Every temp file it writes is named per PR (`/tmp/sova-review-{pr}-{findings,diff,payload}`), because several reviews run concurrently on one machine and the previous shared paths would have paired one PR's findings with another PR's diff, placing inline comments on unrelated lines. `normalize_sha()` and `format_from_data()` were split out of `format_from_json()` so the body and the inline comments are built from one parse of one payload: the non-dict finding filter then applies to both halves, and a body that described a finding the comments had dropped is impossible. Safe to emit these threads because the address cycle already resolves them: `ResolveExternalReviewsStep` passes `ctx.config.github_user` and the active `gh` user alongside `_DEFAULT_CODERABBIT_AUTHORS`, so SOVA's own inline threads are resolved like a bot's (verified on PR #1070: 5 of 5 SOVA threads resolved, 2 of 11 CodeRabbit threads outstanding). Files: `sova/roles/_review_comments.py`, `sova/commands/data/commands/review-pr.md` (mirrored in `.claude/commands/`).

- **Termination provenance is recorded at the point the signal is sent, not guessed at exit**: a run killed by a deliberate stop (dashboard button, watchdog anomaly) and a run killed externally (OOM, `pkill`, a supervisor's SIGTERM) both used to finalize identically as `status="failed"`, making "who killed this run" unanswerable without log archaeology. `TerminationRecord` (`sova/ipc/control.py`, `cause`/`requester`/`signal`/`detail`) is attached to `_BaseAgentProcess` by `stop()` itself, the single choke point every in-process sender (the dashboard's `stop_agent()`, the watchdog's kill path, both routed through the same `stop_agent(cause=..., requester=...)`) already goes through; a `TerminationRecord` present when the process exits means the cause is known locally, regardless of the exact exit code, even across a SIGTERM->SIGKILL escalation (the record's `detail` notes the escalation, but `cause` still names the original deliberate reason). `stop()` returns immediately with no record attached when the process is already not running, since there is nothing to attribute. `normalize_signal_exit()` maps both exit-code conventions a caller might observe to the same signal number: asyncio's own negative form (`-15`) and the shell's `128+signal` form (`143`), so a wrapper layer reporting either shape classifies identically. `_classify_exit_status()` (`sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`) is the decision function `_finalize_task_run()` uses for any nonzero exit: a local `TerminationRecord` always wins (status `"stopped"`, `termination_reason=record.cause`); absent that, a signal-shaped exit means an external kill (status `"interrupted"`, `termination_reason="external_signal"`, with a best-effort `psutil` memory snapshot appended to `error_message` so an OOM kill under pressure can be told apart after the fact from an external stop while the machine was idle); anything else is an ordinary application failure (status `"failed"`, `termination_reason=None`). `TaskRun.termination_reason` (migration `036`) is nullable and every pre-existing row reads `NULL`, which must always be treated as "unknown", never as "external". `"stopped"` is a new terminal status distinct from `"interrupted"` on purpose: `"interrupted"` already means "involuntary" (dead-PID recovery, orphaned runs after a dashboard reload) everywhere else in the codebase, and overloading it would erase the distinction this feature exists to draw. The cost of that choice is real: `TASK_RUN_TERMINAL` is copied rather than imported in four places (`sova/core/workflow.py`'s per-issue budget query, `sova/awareness/providers/agent_runs.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`, `sova/git/worktree.py`'s active-worktree-owner check), and all five copies (plus the canonical `sova.core.state.TASK_RUN_TERMINAL`) needed `"stopped"` added by hand; every other terminal-status consumer imports one of those and picked it up automatically. The merge-role crash-recovery check in `_wait_and_finalize()` (a nonzero exit whose PR turns out to be merged gets upgraded to `status="done", exit_code=0` before `_finalize_task_run()` ever runs) keeps precedence over signal classification for free, since `_classify_exit_status()` is only reached when `exit_code != 0`. Two consumers outside the `TASK_RUN_TERMINAL` family also special-case individual status strings rather than checking set membership, and both needed `"stopped"` added by hand too, the same way the five copies above did: `sova/a2a/task_mapping.py:sova_status_to_a2a()` maps an unrecognized status string to `"failed"` or falls through to `"working"`, so without an explicit branch a `"stopped"` run would have reported as perpetually in-progress to any A2A client polling it (mapped to A2A's `"canceled"` state instead, matching how `a2a_to_sova_status()` already treats an inbound cancel as `REJECTED`); `sova/dashboard/services/lifecycle_service.py:_infer_phase_status()` falls through to `PhaseStatus.ACTIVE` for anything not in its own `("failed", "rejected", "interrupted")` tuple, so the lifecycle view would have shown a stopped run's phase as still active forever. A follow-up review round found the initial fix folded `"stopped"` into the existing `PhaseStatus.FAILED` bucket instead of giving it its own value, which still misrepresented a deliberate stop as an application failure on the one page built to show per-phase status; `_infer_phase_status()` now maps it to a distinct `PhaseStatus.STOPPED`, and `finalize_phase_from_run()` (the non-reconstruction lifecycle path, which previously branched only on `exit_code == 0`) checks `run.status == "stopped"` first via a new `stop_phase()` paralleling `fail_phase()`, since the process can still exit with a nonzero signal-shaped code even though the stop was deliberate. `restart_phase()` and the "Restart Phase" button in `lifecycle.html` both widened from `PhaseStatus.FAILED` only to accept `STOPPED` too, since a stopped phase is exactly as restartable as a failed one. Files: `sova/ipc/control.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_lifecycle.py`, `sova/supervisor/watchdog.py`, `sova/core/state.py`, `sova/db/models.py`, `sova/db/migrations/versions/036_add_task_run_termination_reason.py`, `sova/a2a/task_mapping.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/lifecycle_service.py`, `sova/dashboard/routers/lifecycle.py`, `sova/dashboard/static/app.js`, `sova/dashboard/templates/lifecycle.html`. Issue #978, PR #1100 (CodeRabbit review).
- **The memory guard's runtime check lives in the watchdog's existing poll loop, not a new gate or poller**: `sova/supervisor/gates/memory_pressure.py` only ever evaluates a new spawn (a supervisor progression decision), so pressure that builds up while several agents are already running went unnoticed until something else acted on it. `AgentWatchdog._check_memory_pressure()` extends the existing scan pass instead: it reuses the same `[memory_guard]` thresholds (`enabled`, `block_threshold_gb`), reuses the watchdog's own `WatchdogFinding`/cooldown/`emit_safe` machinery, and fails open on any `psutil` error, exactly like the pre-spawn gate. A new `memory_guard.runtime_action` field (`"warn"` default, or `"stop_newest"`) decides whether pressure only logs a feed event or additionally stops the most-recently-started active run, labelled with cause `"memory_pressure"` so `_classify_exit_status()` records a real capacity reason instead of the run showing up as an unexplained `"external_signal"` interruption. `"warn"` is the default because killing a running agent discards paid work: eviction is opt-in, not automatic. A `WatchdogFinding` for a system-wide condition (no specific run to blame, the `"warn"` case, or `"stop_newest"` with zero active runs) uses a sentinel `run_id=0` (never a real `TaskRun.id`) purely so the existing per-`(run_id, signal)` cooldown mechanism has a key to track; `_prune_cooldowns()` special-cases `run_id == 0` so it survives every cycle's "no longer active" sweep instead of being evicted (and its cooldown defeated) the moment no real run shares that id. `AgentWatchdog` now takes an optional `memory_guard: MemoryGuardConfig` at construction and `reload_config()` gained a second optional parameter so hot-reload (`memory_guard.*` routes to the `"watchdog"` reload target in `sova/dashboard/routers/settings.py`, alongside `watchdog.*`) can update it without a restart. Files: `sova/supervisor/watchdog.py`, `sova/config/models.py`, `sova/dashboard/app.py`, `sova/dashboard/routers/settings.py`, `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`. Issue #978.
- **`sova run` installs its own SIGTERM handler so the grace period before SIGKILL is spent preserving work, not lost to the default disposition**: without a handler, a subprocess spawned by `spawn_direct()` (the pipeline-role path `start_agent()` uses for developer/researcher/planner) is killed outright by the OS on SIGTERM, discarding whatever the running step had staged. `_install_sigterm_handler()` in `sova/cli/commands/run.py` registers `loop.add_signal_handler(signal.SIGTERM, ...)` on the running task right before `dispatch()`, cancelling that task instead of letting the OS terminate the process immediately; `_run_workflow()` catches the resulting `asyncio.CancelledError` around `dispatch()`, runs `_handle_sigterm_shutdown()` inside `asyncio.shield()` (the same shielding rule that protects every other cancellable DB write in this codebase, since an unshielded write here would abandon a SQLite transaction and lock the project database until the process exits), and then re-raises so the process still exits promptly within the parent's `stop()` timeout (default 10s) before SIGKILL. The shutdown handler commits any staged partial work via `commit_partial_work()` (extracted from `WorkflowEngine._preserve_partial_work_on_timeout()` into `sova/git/worktree.py` so both the step-timeout path and the SIGTERM path share one implementation), but only when `ctx.worktree_dir` is already set: before `create_worktree` runs (e.g. `sync`/`assess`, or a role that never creates a worktree), `ctx.working_dir` falls back to `project_dir`, and a dashboard stop, watchdog kill, or the memory-guard eviction below could otherwise commit the operator's own uncommitted edits on whatever branch they have checked out in the primary checkout (CodeRabbit review, PR #1100). When the run is DB-tracked (spawned with `--run-id`), it also best-effort updates `total_cost_usd`; it deliberately never writes `status` itself, since only the parent (via `TerminationRecord` + `_classify_exit_status()`) or the liveness sweep for a standalone run knows the real cause. `run_issue()` maps a `CancelledError` surfacing from `asyncio.run()` to `typer.Exit(128 + SIGTERM)` so a parent observing the CLI's own exit code sees the conventional signal-shaped form even though the handler intercepted the raw signal. `add_signal_handler` is POSIX-only; on a platform where it raises `NotImplementedError`/`RuntimeError`, the fallback is the pre-existing default disposition (immediate termination), i.e. no regression from before this feature existed. Files: `sova/cli/commands/run.py`, `sova/git/worktree.py`, `sova/core/workflow.py`. Issue #978.
- **A cross-vendor credential leak needs an allowlist, not a denylist, of routes that may receive the resolved key**: `LiteLLMProvider` threads a resolved API key into a LiteLLM call via an explicit `api_key=` kwarg (rather than mutating `OPENAI_API_KEY` globally, which would leak it into every other subprocess), but an explicit `api_key=` overrides LiteLLM's own per-provider env resolution, so the key must only be attached when the target model actually routes to the vendor it was resolved for. `create_provider()` only ever resolves `self.api_key` for `self.vendor == "openai"`; a `fallback_model` or `llm.routing.*` entry naming a different vendor (Claude, Gemini, Vertex, Ollama, or any other LiteLLM-routed prefix) must never receive it. The first fix gated attachment with a denylist of five known-foreign prefixes (`azure/`, `groq/`, `mistral/`, `bedrock/`, `openrouter/`), which left every other LiteLLM-routed vendor (`together_ai/`, `deepseek/`, `cohere/`, `claude-`, `anthropic/`, `vertex_ai/`, `gemini/`, `ollama/`, ...) exposed to the same leak. `_model_matches_key_vendor()` (`sova/llm/litellm_provider.py`) instead allowlists: a bare model id (no `"<provider>/"` prefix) routes to OpenAI by LiteLLM's default and matches, an explicit `openai/` prefix matches, and every other prefixed id does not. Only `self.vendor == "openai"` is checked at all; every other vendor keeps the prior unconditional behavior, since a caller that constructs the provider directly with its own explicit `api_key=` (e.g. a custom `vendor="litellm"` endpoint) still expects it forwarded to every call that provider makes. File: `sova/llm/litellm_provider.py`. Issue #1148, PR #1153.
- **A headless server has no display to open a browser on, and the UI must detect that before offering a browser-based reconnect action**: the Connections page's `claude auth login` reconnect button assumed an interactive desktop session with a browser available to complete the OAuth flow; on a headless server (SSH session, container, systemd service) that button opens nothing and leaves the operator stuck. `_is_headless_environment()` (`sova/dashboard/services/setup_service.py`) follows POSIX convention only: a GUI session sets `DISPLAY` (X11) or `WAYLAND_DISPLAY` (Wayland), and `BROWSER` is an explicit operator override; absence of all three means headless. Always assumed available (non-headless) on non-POSIX platforms, where that convention does not apply and no equivalent signal is checked. The result is folded into a reason string (`"headless" | "vertex_routed" | "bedrock_routed"`, plus a detail) so the reconnect widget can tell a true headless host apart from a Vertex/Bedrock-redirected CLI that also can't use `claude auth login` but for an unrelated reason. `connections.html` suppresses the browser-opening reconnect button in favor of a terminal-fallback instruction: authenticate via `claude auth login` on the server itself (a terminal session that host already has), then click Validate to recheck, rather than advising the operator to copy credential files between machines, which would spread a credential outside the keyring/DB boundary every other provider's credential handling in this codebase stays inside. Files: `sova/dashboard/services/setup_service.py`, `sova/dashboard/templates/connections.html`. PR #1153.
- **An unassigned ticket's suitability lives in a local record, never a label its owner's account cannot speak for**: `/find-task` triages both self-assigned and unassigned tickets into AUTO/INTERACTIVE/BLOCKED/CLOSE, but writing the matching `agent:ready`/`agent:interactive-recommended`/`agent:human-only` tracker label on a ticket nobody has assigned to this account overstates what the account is entitled to declare on the tracker. The write-back splits by assignee instead: a self-assigned ticket still gets the label (unchanged from before), while every AUTO/INTERACTIVE/BLOCKED ticket, self-assigned or not, also gets a row in the `task_assessments` table via the new `sova assess set <issue> <ready|interactive|human-only>` CLI command (`sova/cli/commands/assess.py`, backed by `sova/knowledge/assessments.py`). The table is append-only (`get` and the gate both read the most recently `assessed_at` row for a given `issue_number` + `project_slug`), so a re-run of `/find-task` never needs to delete anything first; `sova assess clear` exists for the CLI surface but the supervisor itself never calls it. `check_human_involvement_gate()` (`sova/supervisor/gates/human_involvement.py`) is the sole consumer on the read side: it still blocks on the two suitability labels first, and only when none of the three labels (including `agent:ready`, which short-circuits the fallback instead of letting a stale local `human_only` row re-block a ticket a human has since re-labeled) is present does it query the local record as a fallback, so a label always wins over a conflicting local row in both directions. Unlike a tracker label, the local record never self-clears (nothing calls `sova assess clear` on an issue's behalf), so the fallback is additionally scoped to the `candidate` the caller passes: only `spawn_triage`/`spawn_researcher`/`spawn_developer` (pre-development work, where a block is "don't start this yet") ever consult it, and any later pipeline-state label (`agent:triaged`, `agent:researched`, `agent:in-progress`, `agent:in-review`, `agent:on-qa`) short-circuits it the same way `agent:ready` does, as defense in depth. Without that scoping an unassigned ticket a human develops manually after `/find-task` marked it `human_only`/`interactive_recommended` would have its PR permanently refused address-review and integrate, since nothing on the finishing side ever clears the record; `TaskProgressionEngine._collect_gate_blockers()` passes `candidate.value` through for exactly this reason. The local-record lookup is wrapped in a broad `except Exception` that logs and returns "not blocked": an unreadable or misconfigured local store must fail open the same way every other "could not verify" condition in this codebase does, rather than silently blocking a ticket that was never meant to be gated this way. `TaskProgressionEngine._collect_gate_blockers()` passes its own `session_factory` and `self._config.github_repo` as `project_slug` through to the gate, matching the `project_slug` convention used elsewhere in the supervisor. The gate is the only read-side consumer of the local record by design: `dependency_graph.is_ready()` (the allow-direction counterpart used by `is_human_only`/`is_interactive_recommended`) has no local-record equivalent, and `get_ready_tasks()` (issue #1227: now routed through `assess_suitability()`, so it excludes `epic`/`human`/`interactive` labels and open dependencies, not state alone) still consults no local record, since it calls `assess_suitability()` with `local_assessment=None`; a `ready` record stays informational, not gating, because absence of a blocking label or record already means "not blocked," so there is nothing for an allow-direction consumer to add. That call is one of four coroutines in `_collect_gate_blockers()`'s existing `asyncio.gather`, not a separate `await` ahead of it: the function runs once per issue per supervisor cycle, and the gate's lookup is independent of the three gates already gathered there. Two details of the write side exist because the `task_assessments` table is older than this feature and had no producer in `sova/` before it. First, `set_assessment()` writes `estimated_complexity=""` rather than letting the column fall to its `"moderate"` default: `GET /api/agents/issue/{n}/complexity` reads the latest row's `estimated_complexity` and, when truthy, returns it instead of calling `assess_complexity()`, so a default-valued column would have pinned the launch modal's model suggestion to the moderate tier for every ticket `/find-task` triaged, permanently shadowing the on-the-fly path that branch had always fallen through to. Second, `sova assess set` resolves its project directory with `get_primary_worktree_root()` (as `sova run` does) rather than a bare `Path.cwd()`: `_get_database_url()` appends `.claude/sova.db` to whatever directory it is handed with no git-common-dir resolution, so a write issued from a linked worktree would land in a throwaway DB that the supervisor never reads and that is deleted with the worktree. It also rejects an issue argument that does not normalize to a bare number, since the gate looks the row up by the issue's integer id and a row keyed by anything else can never be read, and rejects a tracker-key prefix that does not match the configured project (`task_source.jira_project_key`, or the repo part of `github_repo`), since `RHCLOUD-1234` and `ABC-1234` both normalize to the same `1234` and would otherwise misattribute a foreign ticket's verdict to this project's issue of the same number. `--project` additionally requires an existing directory (`exists=True, file_okay=False`) that already looks like an installed project (an existing `.claude/sova.db`, an unmigrated `sova.toml`, or a registry entry): without that check a mistyped path would silently run every migration and create a throwaway DB, reporting success for a record no supervisor will ever read. `sova assess get` prints its value with `typer.echo` (stdout), mirroring `sova config get`'s stream contract, and reserves the stderr `Console` for the status/diagnostic lines in `set`/`clear`, so `VAL=$(sova assess get N)` captures the value rather than an empty string. Files: `sova/cli/commands/assess.py`, `sova/knowledge/assessments.py`, `sova/supervisor/gates/human_involvement.py`, `sova/supervisor/progression.py`, `sova/commands/data/commands/find-task.md`. Issue #1198.
- **A status broadcast producer that only selects non-terminal rows can never carry a terminal transition for the client to act on**: `get_all_agent_statuses()` queries `TaskRun.status.notin_(_TERMINAL_STATUSES)` by construction, so the periodic `status_update` WebSocket broadcast it feeds can never include a run that just finished with its terminal status; a terminal-state browser notification that watches for a status transition inside that broadcast's handler is unreachable dead code by construction, not merely untested. `_ConnectionManager.broadcast_event()` (`sova/dashboard/routers/agents.py`) gained an `extra: dict | None` parameter so a one-shot event can carry payload fields beyond `{"type": event_type}`; `_announce_agent_finished()` (`sova/dashboard/services/agent_finalize.py`) uses it to attach `run_id`/`status`/`issue`/`pr_number`/`role` to the `agent_finished` event, which already fires exactly once per exit regardless of role or spawn path. The broadcast `status` is read back from the DB via `_get_task_run_status()` (`sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`) immediately before the broadcast, not the exit-code-derived local variable: a deliberate stop, an external signal, or a run that was already terminal before this finalize call (rejected, paused, awaiting_approval) all diverge from a bare "done"/"failed" guess, and broadcasting the guess made the notification report the wrong outcome in exactly those routine cases. `agents.html`'s notification now fires from `agent_finished`, reading `msg.status` directly, instead of diffing a per-run status history (`_wsPreviousStatuses`) that could never see a terminal value to diff against. Delivery itself depends on both sides of `_ConnectionManager` keying on the same project_dir form: `ws_agent_status()` registers under `get_project_dir() or get_default_project_dir()` (the websocket scope never gets `ProjectContextMiddleware`'s contextvar, which only HTTP requests under `/p/` receive), while a broadcaster can reach the same project through a different, unresolved form (e.g. `AgentState.project_dir`'s `default_factory=Path.cwd`). `_normalize_project_key()` resolves every key on both the `connect`/`disconnect`/`broadcast_event` entry points, so the two sides land in the same group instead of silently landing in two, one of which has no subscribers (empirically confirmed to drop every `agent_finished` and `graph_invalidated` message in a single-project deployment before this fix). A sustained run of `status_unavailable` messages (the producer's own fetch-failure signal) is tracked with a streak counter and, after a few consecutive misses, surfaces a toast once and starts a real repeating fallback poll (`_wsUnavailablePollInterval`, a `setInterval`, not a one-shot debounced refresh) that keeps running for the rest of the outage: a single miss is normal and ignored, but the socket staying open while delivering nothing forever otherwise freezes the grid with no visible signal, since the reconnect path never engages. Only a `status_update` (the producer actually recovering) clears the streak and stops the fallback interval; an `agent_finished` arriving mid-outage must not reset it, or a batch of agents exiting during an outage would repeatedly postpone detection. `createSovaStatusChannel()` (`sova/dashboard/static/app.js`) separately gained a `staleAfterMs` liveness watchdog (default `max(20000, 10x pollIntervalMs)`, deliberately generous rather than `3x pollIntervalMs`: the producer's actual inter-message gap is its broadcast interval plus however long its server-side status fetch takes, which is unbounded and independent of the client's poll cadence, so a tighter default misdiagnoses a slow-but-alive server as a dead socket) that force-closes a socket that has delivered nothing for that long, routing into the existing `onclose` -> fallback/reconnect path: the prior reconnect-counter reset on a parsed message only prevented backoff buildup on a flapping connection, and did nothing for a socket that stays open but goes silent (a dead proxy, a half-open connection after sleep/network-switch, a server hung on a locked DB). `agents.html` passes an explicit `staleAfterMs: 20000` tied to the known 2s server broadcast interval rather than relying on the generic default. Cost serialization is unified behind one `format_cost_usd()` helper (`sova/utils/formatting.py`, next to `decimal_to_json`) used at every cost site (`agent_status.py`'s WS and REST paths, `agent_db._fetch_run_states`, and the pool-tracked/completed branches in `agent_lifecycle.py`): calling `decimal_to_json` directly on a cost field answered "0.00" for a NULL `total_cost_usd` (a pre-migration row SQLite backfills as NULL regardless of the model's Python-side default) while every other branch answered "0.000000", contradicting `AgentStatus`'s own claim that the WS and REST shapes never disagree on a field's meaning. `supervisor.html` still runs its own, separate hand-rolled WebSocket client against the same `/ws/agents/status` endpoint (no reconnect cap, no fallback, no stale-socket detection) and was out of scope for this migration; it is a known follow-up alongside `dashboard.html`/`run_detail.html` under epic #1049. Files: `sova/dashboard/routers/agents.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_finalize.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_status.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_lifecycle.py`, `sova/utils/formatting.py`, `sova/dashboard/static/app.js`, `sova/dashboard/templates/agents.html`. Issue #1051.

- **A tool-using step must never silently degrade to a text-only LLM call**: `DevelopStep` (and any future step that edits the working tree) calls `sova.core.agent_dispatch.dispatch_command()` instead of `sova.llm.client.invoke_command()` directly. `BaseStep.requires_tools` (default `False`) is the step's own declaration that it needs a real coding-agent runtime, not a provider capability: some `LLMProvider` backends (LiteLLM, the Anthropic API) are pure text-completion endpoints with no file-editing mechanism at all, so routing a tool-using step to one of them previously didn't fail, it just produced a chat response with zero changes, discovered only later (and expensively) by `validate_output()`'s diff check. `dispatch_command()` checks `step.requires_tools` first: `False` delegates to `client.invoke_command()` unchanged (every non-editing step, e.g. triage/research/review, is unaffected); `True` always routes through `sova.ipc.runtime.get_runtime()` instead, regardless of which `LLMProvider` the project configured, and raises `AgentRuntimeUnavailableError` immediately when that runtime's CLI is missing or not runnable rather than falling back to a text-only call that cannot do the work (the same no-implicit-fallback rule already established for model selection). This check does not probe authentication: `check_available()` is implemented per runtime (today only `CodexRuntime` checks login state), so an installed-but-unauthenticated Claude Code CLI passes this gate and fails later, inside the spawned process. For the tool path it also resolves the command's canonical markdown body (stripping YAML frontmatter) and spawns it as a literal prompt, since a runtime with no slash-command concept of its own (Codex) cannot resolve a bare `"/develop"` reference the way the Claude Code CLI can. Taking over prompt assembly means taking over the CLI's `$ARGUMENTS` substitution with it: `_assemble_prompt()` replaces the placeholder in place when the body carries one (`.claude/commands/develop.md` puts it under "**Task to develop**:", and later numbered steps refer back to it) and only appends when it does not. Appending unconditionally would leave the agent reading a literal `$ARGUMENTS` where its task should be, with every back-reference to that placeholder dangling: a silently degraded prompt rather than a failure, on the default `claude-code` runtime as much as on Codex. The tool path also has to run the pre-flight the `invoke*` entry points run, since it no longer passes through any of them: `client.prepare_invocation()` (extracted from `invoke()`/`invoke_command()`, which now call it too) counts the call against `runaway.max_llm_calls` and rejects it when that ceiling is reached, applies the `llm.routing[task_type]` route (which outranks the `model=` every pipeline step passes, so dropping it would silently disable routing for the one step that most needs it), resolves the result through `resolve_alias()` (without which a bare tier name reaches a Vertex/Bedrock deployment that cannot serve it, the #1033 failure mode), and defaults the timeout from config instead of waiting unbounded. `dispatch_prompt()` is the raw-prompt sibling for a tool-using step that assembles its own prompt rather than naming a command: `DevelopStep`'s inner fix loop, which asks the agent to edit source files until the project's check command passes, is as tool-dependent as the `/develop` dispatch it follows, so leaving it on `client.invoke()` would have reintroduced the same silent degradation one call later. `_run_agent_process()` mirrors `LLMProvider.invoke()`'s failure contract so existing step-layer `except RuntimeError` fix-loop handling needs no AgentRuntime-specific branch: a timeout raises `LLMTimeoutError` (not a bare failure), any other non-zero exit is run through `classify_error()` on the captured stderr, and every way out of the wait, not only `TimeoutError`/`CancelledError`, stops the spawned process before propagating (`_stop_quietly()` swallows a failure from the stop itself, except `CancelledError`, so a kill failure never masks the real cause) so a step failure of any kind never leaves an orphaned agent subprocess behind. A terminal result event that reports a failed turn (`LLMResult.is_error`, set from the Claude CLI's `is_error`/non-`"success"` `subtype` via `_claude_result_stop_reason()` in `sova/ipc/runtime.py`, mirroring how `CodexStreamParser` already set it) is raised through `classify_error()` rather than returned, so the step cannot mistake a reported failure for a successful empty turn. The tool path also shares the `invoke*` path's model-fallback walk and budget-cap pre-check rather than bypassing them: `sova.llm.client.walk_model_chain()` is `_invoke_with_fallback()`'s chain-walking core extracted to take an already-bound `attempt` callable instead of fetching a provider itself, so `_dispatch_to_runtime()` in `agent_dispatch.py` can bind an `AgentRuntime` into it the same way the provider path binds an `LLMProvider`, which keeps `agent.fallback_models` effective for a tool-using step. `AgentRuntime.enforces_budget_cap` (default `False`, `True` on `ClaudeCodeRuntime` since `spawn()` passes `max_budget_usd` to the CLI's own `--max-budget-usd`) is the runtime-side counterpart of `ProviderCapabilities.supports_budget_cap`: `_raise_if_budget_cap_unsupported()` in `agent_dispatch.py` raises `BillingError` before spawning a non-enforcing runtime (Codex, Aider) with a non-positive remaining budget, instead of letting it spend with no limit. Every other step that mutates the working tree or rewrites history is migrated the same way: `ValidateStep` and `MonitorCIStep` set `requires_tools = True` and route their LLM fix loops (invoked via an injected callable in `MonitorCIStep`'s case, so the existing `_invoke_fix()` unit tests keep passing a bare mock) through `dispatch_prompt()`; `SelfReviewStep`, `SimplifyStep`, and `RearrangeCommitsStep` route their named `/review`, `/simplify`, and `/rearrange-commits` invocations through `dispatch_command()`; `AddressReviewStep` routes through `dispatch_prompt()`, not `dispatch_command()`, because its payload is a step-assembled findings prompt rather than a named slash command, same as `DevelopStep`'s inner fix loop. `ResearchStep` is the one deliberate holdout: `/research` never touches the git worktree, only appends a `## Research` section to the issue/ticket body via `gh`/`jira` CLI calls, and its own `validate_output()` already gates on that section existing in the tracker regardless of which provider produced it, so it stays on `invoke_command()`/LLMProvider. Files: `sova/core/agent_dispatch.py`, `sova/core/steps/base.py`, `sova/core/steps/develop.py`, `sova/core/steps/validate.py`, `sova/core/steps/monitor_ci.py`, `sova/core/steps/self_review.py`, `sova/core/steps/simplify.py`, `sova/core/steps/address_review.py`, `sova/core/steps/address_external_findings.py`, `sova/core/steps/rearrange_commits.py`, `sova/core/steps/research.py`, `sova/llm/client.py`, `sova/llm/provider.py`, `sova/ipc/runtime.py`. Issue #1125, PR #1167.
- **A generated PR title's scope must come from the same source as a commit message's scope, or only one of them stays valid when the format rule changes: an issue number is not a scope**: `_build_pr_title()` previously emitted `feat(#NNNN): <description>` for every GitHub PR, placing the issue number where `invariants/commit-format.sh`'s `VALID_SCOPES` check expects a real scope (`core`, `dashboard`, `cli`, ...). `/integrate-pr`'s Phase 2b silent-revert gate synthesizes a trial-merge commit whose subject is the PR title and parses that subject's scope to decide whether a large deletion is exempt from the revert check; a `#NNNN` scope parses as `none`, which carries no exemption, so any PR with a sizable legitimate deletion was flagged as a false-positive silent revert (confirmed on PR #1239 and #1240, each needing a manual title rename before the gate would pass). The JIRA title branch (`[KEY-N] feat: ...`) and generated commit messages (`sova/core/steps/commit.py`'s `_normalize_commit_subject()`, `feat(core): ...`) were never affected: both already used a real scope or a bracketed ticket key, which is what exposed the GitHub-title branch as the one inconsistent path. The fix adds `_infer_scope()`: `CreatePRStep._resolve_changed_files()` runs `git diff {base}...HEAD --name-only` (the merge-base, three-dot form, not `{base}..HEAD`: a two-dot diff can pull in base-only paths once the base branch advances after this branch diverges, which could outnumber the PR's own paths and select the wrong scope; skipped entirely for JIRA, which does not infer a scope, and fails open to an empty list on a git error so a diff failure never blocks PR creation), and `_infer_scope()` maps each `sova/<dir>/...` path to `<dir>` when it is a member of `_VALID_SCOPES`, returning the most-touched scope (ties break to the first scope seen, for determinism) and falling back to `core` for mixed, non-`sova`, or empty changes: a single path outside `sova/<valid-scope>/` forces the fallback for the whole change, even when every other path is validly scoped. The issue number is dropped from the title entirely rather than relocated: it was already linked via `Closes #N` in the PR body (`_PR_BODY_ISSUE_SECTION_GITHUB`), so the title never needed it, and the dashboard's `_extract_linked_issue()` (`sova/dashboard/services/pr_service.py`) only reads a title's `#N` as a fallback after the body, so dropping it from newly-created titles does not break parsing of PRs that still carry the legacy form. `_VALID_SCOPES` is a second hardcoded copy of the invariant's scope list (duplicating it rather than shelling out to parse the bash script at runtime), so a `TestValidScopesNoDrift` test reads `VALID_SCOPES="..."` directly out of `invariants/commit-format.sh` and asserts the two sets are identical, catching drift the moment either list changes without the other. Files: `sova/core/steps/create_pr.py`, `tests/test_core.py`, `tests/test_create_pr_coverage.py`. Issue #1245, PR #1239, #1240.
- **The three suitability labels are mutually exclusive, so every producer applies them through one helper, and a config pattern match is never re-litigated by the model**: `TaskAssessment.suitability` gained a fifth value, `interactive`, mapped to `agent:interactive-recommended` (issue #1229), which `check_human_involvement_gate()` already treats as a hard block on autonomous scheduling. That made two pre-existing shapes load-bearing. First, `agent:ready`, `agent:interactive-recommended` and `agent:human-only` answer the same question and cannot coexist, but no triage write path ever called `remove_label()` for them (only `agent_recovery.py`'s triage rollback did, and it strips every `agent:` label wholesale, so it needed no change for the new value): four independent call sites (`TriageRole.execute()`, `sova/cli/commands/triage.py:_apply_triage_actions()`, and the batch-triage and harden-retriage paths in `sova/dashboard/services/batch_service.py`) each duplicated `resolve_label()` plus `add_label()`, so a re-triage that changed the verdict left both the old and the new label on the issue, and `check_human_involvement_gate()`'s fixed check order (human_only, then interactive_recommended, then ready) meant a stale blocking label permanently outranked the newer `agent:ready` verdict. All four now call `TriageRole.apply_suitability_label()`, which adds the resolved label first and only then strips whichever of the other two the issue currently carries, re-fetching the issue's labels immediately before that removal rather than trusting a snapshot taken before the (potentially tens-of-seconds) LLM round trip, since a label change made during that window would otherwise be invisible; a failed add leaves the previous, more conservative label in place instead of the issue briefly carrying no suitability label at all, and a failed removal is logged and left for the next retriage to clean up. When `resolve_label()` returns `None` because `triage.labels` opted that outcome out of labelling entirely (an empty-string override), `apply_suitability_label()` adds nothing but still strips stale labels for outcomes strictly less conservative than the one opted out (ranked `ready` < `interactive` < `human_only`): an opted-out `interactive` verdict still clears a stale `agent:ready`, since that label is now wrong regardless of whether the operator wants the correct one written, but it never touches a more conservative `agent:human-only`, since an unlabelled outcome can never be the authority for erasing a stronger block. This mirrors `ReviewerRole`'s existing `_VERDICT_TO_LABEL` strip-the-others-then-add loop and `/find-task`'s own sequential add-plus-remove write-back rather than `_set_state_label()`'s atomic full-array PATCH (the suitability labels are not state labels, and `execute()` already applies them with independent calls). The issue's own human-only label is checked ahead of the `interactive_title_prefixes`/`interactive_labels` config patterns in `heuristic_assess()`, so a human's hard block can never be downgraded by a title match (`interactive_title_prefixes` defaults to an empty list, like `skip_title_prefixes`; `["[Spike]"]` is README's suggested value, not a shipped default, so no existing install is reclassified on upgrade without an explicit config change). That check resolves the label through `triage.labels` (`self.resolve_label("human_only", triage_cfg)`) rather than a hardcoded literal, so an install that renames the label via config is still protected; the hardcoded `is_human_only()` check still runs alongside it as defense in depth for a partially-overridden label map. `heuristic_assess()` gives the `interactive` label the same sticky treatment: an issue already carrying the resolved interactive label is returned as `interactive` before the content heuristic ever runs, since a prior interactive verdict (written by a human or by `/find-task`) is a signal the content heuristic has no way to recompute on its own and would otherwise silently downgrade to `ready` on the next re-triage. The quality gate (`_apply_quality_gate()`) similarly lets an `interactive`/`human_only` LLM verdict win ahead of both the enrichment-success and `needs_spec` fallback paths: fixing the body's structure does not make a ticket that needs a human or an interactive session autonomous.
