The enrichment write (`edit_body()` with the rewritten body) is deferred to `execute()`'s own later `write_body` append rather than written twice (once here, once there) whenever that append is actually going to happen (`triage_cfg.mode != "comment" and triage_cfg.write_body`); `_apply_quality_gate()` writes immediately only for `mode == "comment"` or `write_body == False`, the two cases where `execute()` itself never writes the body at all. Either way, a successful rewrite is kept regardless of which verdict wins instead of being discarded; `_apply_quality_gate()` returns the written body and its re-scored `QualityScore` alongside the assessment so `execute()` recomputes `quality` from the body it is actually about to use, rather than reporting the stale pre-enrichment score in both the appended `## Triage Assessment` section and the run summary. `needs_spec`/`needs_research` are deliberately outside that exclusion set: they describe what the issue is missing, not whether it is pickable, so applying one strips the three pickability labels but applying a pickability label leaves them alone. Second, `assess_tasks_batch()` short-circuited the Batch API call on `human_only` only. A ticket matched by the new `triage.interactive_title_prefixes`/`interactive_labels` config lists would otherwise have been sent to the model anyway and had the operator's deterministic verdict overwritten by whatever the model replied, so that check now tests `_LLM_SHORT_CIRCUIT_SUITABILITIES` (`human_only` plus `interactive`; a config pattern match and an issue's own pre-existing suitability label are both deterministic signals this gates, not only a config pattern, hence the outcome-based name). That same set has to gate the Batch API's own failure path too: `invoke_batch()` raising must not hand the short-circuited tickets back to `_sequential_fallback()` along with the LLM-bound ones, or the model re-decides a verdict it was never supposed to see. `assess_tasks_batch()` tracks exactly which tasks were turned into `BatchRequest`s and, on failure, runs the fallback only over that subset, merging its results back with the already-computed heuristic ones by original index; `_sequential_fallback()` also re-checks `heuristic_assess()` itself before calling the LLM, as defense in depth for any other caller. The same value had to be added to `sova/core/schemas/triage_assessment.json`'s `suitability` enum, not just the `Literal`: `assess_task_with_llm()` validates through that schema, and an omitted enum member silently discards a correct `interactive` reply and falls back to heuristics. Files: `sova/roles/triage.py`, `sova/roles/base.py`, `sova/config/models.py`, `sova/core/schemas/triage_assessment.json`, `sova/cli/commands/triage.py`, `sova/cli/commands/harden.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/batch_service.py`, `sova/dashboard/settings_meta.py`, `sova/commands/data/commands/find-task.md` (and its rendered `.claude/commands/find-task.md`/`.agents/skills/sova-find-task/SKILL.md` copies), `README.md`. Issue #1229.
- **One shared suitability classifier, and the two ways routing a decision through it can weaken it**: `DependencyGraph.get_ready_tasks()`, `queue_maintenance._discover_ready()` and `check_human_involvement_gate()` each re-derived their own subset of "is this issue fit for an agent", so the graph, the queue auto-fill and the gate could disagree about the same issue. `sova/supervisor/suitability.py:assess_suitability(task, graph=None, local_assessment=None, *, consider_dependencies=True)` is now the single answer: a `SuitabilityMode` of `epic` > `human` > `blocked` > `interactive` > `autonomous` > `unclassified`, with a tracker label always beating a conflicting local `task_assessments` record in both directions, plus the `reasons` and `blocked_by` detail every node of `/api/dependencies/graph` now carries. Two non-obvious consequences of having one ordered chain instead of three independent checks. (1) **A caller that acts on only part of the chain must not be given a real graph.** `blocked` outranks `interactive`, and the human-involvement gate deliberately does not act on `blocked` (dependency blocking is `check_dependency_gate()`'s job, config-gated by `supervisor.respect_dependencies`), so passing the supervisor's real graph into that gate silently reclassified an `agent:interactive-recommended` issue that also had an open dependency as `blocked` and the gate then let it through, defeating the label entirely. The gate therefore calls `assess_suitability(task, consider_dependencies=False, local_assessment=...)`, which skips the dependency branch (and the graph construction it would need) entirely rather than being fed a stand-in graph: `graph=None` and `consider_dependencies=False` together mean "no dependency data by design," distinct from `graph=None` with the default `consider_dependencies=True`, which means dependency data was supposed to resolve and didn't, and fails closed. An earlier revision used a `labels_only_graph(task)` sentinel object for the same purpose; it was removed because `DependencyGraph.__init__` does a bare `int(t.id)` and crashed on a non-numeric (Jira-style) task id, and because a sentinel-graph and a "data unavailable" `None` were too easy to confuse at a new call site. `tests/test_progression.py::TestHumanInvolvementGate::test_gate_takes_no_dependency_graph` pins the gate's own signature; `test_non_numeric_issue_id_does_not_crash` pins the Jira-id case. (2) **Fail-closed on unresolvable dependencies is only safe when the graph can actually resolve them.** `assess_suitability` treats a dependency it cannot find as blocking, which is right for `build_dependency_graph()`'s output (it fetches `state="all"` and resolves out-of-page references individually) and catastrophic for a graph built from `queue_maintenance`'s own `adapter.list_tasks()` call, which defaults to open issues only: a dependency closed *because it is done* is simply absent there, so every issue that ever listed a dependency reads as `blocked` and the queue silently stops filling. `maintain_queue()` therefore builds the real graph via `build_dependency_graph(adapter)` (cached per repo for 180s, so the supervisor's own later build in the same poll cycle reuses it rather than paying twice), but only when `supervisor.respect_dependencies` is true, there is queue capacity, and at least one RESEARCHED issue outside the queue to classify (`_ready_candidate_ids()`, which also drives the local-assessment lookup below), and only after the same rate-limit and connectivity gates `ProgressionEngine.evaluate_all()` checks (in the same order: connectivity, then rate limit) before its own build, so a cooldown, an outage, or the switch costs an in-memory check rather than a wasted fetch; the queue auto-fill is an *exclusive* filter (an issue withheld here never reaches `TaskProgressionEngine._resolve_task_ids()` at all), so it has to honour the same config switch as `check_dependency_gate()` or the switch stops doing what it says for issues that never made it into the queue. `_discover_ready()` calls `assess_suitability(task, graph, local_assessment=..., consider_dependencies=graph.has_task(issue_id))` per issue, so a build failure (or the config switch), or an issue missing from the graph, fails open (labels still apply, dependencies don't) rather than crashing or blocking; an issue present in the graph but whose dependency reference the graph cannot resolve fails *closed*, exactly as `DependencyGraph.get_ready_tasks()` does, so the two consumers always agree rather than the queue silently re-admitting an issue the graph API still reports as `blocked`. The `local_assessment` passed in is resolved once per poll cycle via `_resolve_local_assessments()` against a `session_factory` the caller passes in (mirroring the gate's own parameter rather than deriving one from `project_dir`, which in a test is rarely a real initialized database): without it, an issue with only a local `human_only`/`interactive_recommended` record (no tracker label) would have been queued as `unclassified` and then blocked forever by the gate, permanently occupying a queue slot. Every exclusion logs via `log_once()` (`sova/utils/log_dedup.py`, which `warn_once()` now wraps) at info level keyed by `{issue_id}:{mode}` so a dropped issue is never silently missing from the queue, without the steady-state issue re-logging every ~2-minute poll cycle forever. The gate also gained an `epic` block, which nothing had before: an epic is a tracking container and no role should be spawned on one; the gate's `_BLOCKS` lookup table is now paired with an explicit `_BLOCKING_MODES` set so a future mode that becomes blocking but has no mapped `_BLOCKS` entry logs a warning and still blocks, rather than silently falling through the `.get()` default into "allow," and the local-record DB lookup is now skipped (not just deferred) when a blocking label is already present, restoring the short-circuit the original label-only checks had. The label predicates (`is_epic`/`is_human_only`/`is_interactive_recommended`/`is_ready`) and `extract_priority()` live in `sova/supervisor/labels.py`, a leaf module with no imports of `dependency_graph` or `suitability`, rather than in `dependency_graph.py` as an earlier revision had them (or in `suitability.py`, as the issue's spec first proposed): either of those would have forced one of `dependency_graph.py`/`suitability.py` to import the other only inside function bodies to dodge a cycle, since `DependencyGraph.get_ready_tasks()`/`to_dict()` call `assess_suitability()` and `assess_suitability()`'s own dependency walk needs `DependencyGraph` (now only as a `TYPE_CHECKING` annotation). `dependency_graph.py` re-exports the four label predicates and `extract_priority()` for existing callers. The three stored `task_assessments.suitability` values are pinned in `suitability.py` to `sova.knowledge.assessments.SUITABILITY_SHORTHANDS` (its sole writer) rather than duplicated as bare string literals, with a test asserting the two sets match, so a rename on that side fails CI instead of silently falling through to `unclassified`. Files: `sova/supervisor/suitability.py`, `sova/supervisor/labels.py`, `sova/supervisor/dependency_graph.py`, `sova/supervisor/queue_maintenance.py`, `sova/supervisor/gates/human_involvement.py`, `sova/utils/log_dedup.py`. Issue #1227.

Issue #1227.

`AGENT_ELIGIBLE_MODES` includes `unclassified` alongside `autonomous` (pinned by `test_agent_eligible_modes_are_exactly_autonomous_and_unclassified`), which is a deliberate deviation from issue #1227's acceptance criteria text ("`get_ready_tasks()` returns only `autonomous` issues"): most issues on a real tracker carry no `agent:ready` label at all, so a strict `autonomous`-only reading would empty both the graph's `ready` list and the queue auto-fill for every unlabelled issue. The issue's own text was never amended to match; this entry is the record of record for the chosen semantics.

- **A caller-side local-assessment fallback must honour the same scoping rule everywhere it is read**: the entry above's rationale for feeding `local_assessment` into `_discover_ready()` was backwards for the common case. On GitHub, `TaskState.RESEARCHED` is derived solely from the `agent:researched` label, so every real RESEARCHED candidate `_discover_ready()` classifies already carries that label; `check_human_involvement_gate()` treats `agent:researched` (and `agent:triaged`/`agent:in-progress`/`agent:in-review`/`agent:on-qa`) as "past pre-development" and never consults a local `task_assessments` record once one of those labels is present (the fallback exists only so a record can gate *starting* work, never block an already-started PR from finishing). The queue's first version of the local-assessment feed skipped that scoping and applied the record unconditionally, so a stale local `human_only`/`interactive_recommended` record left behind by `/find-task` for a ticket a human had since triaged and researched was withheld from the queue forever, while the gate itself would have let the same issue through: the inverse of the disagreement issue #1227 set out to remove. The two unit/integration tests that exercised this path (`test_local_human_only_record_excludes_candidate`, `TestMaintainQueueLocalAssessment`) built `Task(state=RESEARCHED, labels=[])`, a combination the GitHub adapter can never produce, which is why the bug was invisible; both now also cover the realistic `labels=["agent:researched"]` case, pinning that a pipeline-state label supersedes the record. Fix: `is_past_pre_development()`/`local_assessment_applies()` moved out of `gates/human_involvement.py` into the leaf module `sova/supervisor/labels.py` (not into the classifier itself: `assess_suitability()` stays general-purpose, and scoping remains a caller-side concern per the original design, now just a *shared* caller-side predicate instead of two independently-maintained copies). `check_human_involvement_gate()`'s DB-lookup guard and `queue_maintenance._discover_ready()`/`_prune_unsuitable()` all call the one `local_assessment_applies(labels)` before trusting a resolved record, so the gate, the queue auto-fill, and a precomputed map handed into `_discover_ready()` from a test cannot disagree about when a record still applies.

- **Readiness re-checking must cover entries already in the queue, not just new candidates, and the local-assessment lookup must be batched and capacity-gated like the graph build**: `queue_maintenance._prune_done()` only ever removed `DONE`/`HUMAN_ONLY` entries, so an issue already sitting in the persisted queue that later acquired `type: epic`, `agent:interactive-recommended`, or a local human-only/interactive record (readiness is not re-evaluated once an issue is admitted) stayed there forever: re-blocked by `check_human_involvement_gate()` on every poll cycle while still occupying a `max_queue_size` slot `_discover_ready()` could no longer fill. `_prune_unsuitable()` closes this by re-running `assess_suitability()` over every already-queued id still in `TaskState.RESEARCHED` (deliberately *not* over `IN_PROGRESS`/later-state entries: an issue an agent is already developing must never be pulled out of the queue because its suitability drifted mid-flight) and dropping any that no longer classify `autonomous`/`unclassified`, logging each removal via `log_once()` exactly like `_discover_ready()`'s own exclusions. `maintain_queue()` now resolves local assessments for the union of new RESEARCHED candidates (only when there is queue capacity to add them) and already-queued RESEARCHED entries (always, since pruning is not capacity-gated and frees a slot the same cycle), filtered down first to ids where `local_assessment_applies()` is true, so a candidate a label already decides is never queried for in the first place. That lookup itself moved from one `get_latest_assessment()` call per candidate to a single batched `get_latest_assessments(session, issue_numbers=[...], project_slug=...)` (`sova/knowledge/assessments.py`): one query ordered `assessed_at desc, id desc` with the first row kept per `issue_number`, rather than an N+1 round trip on every ~2-minute poll cycle for a backlog that could hold tens of RESEARCHED issues. The graph-build guard also widened to cover pruning: it now builds whenever there is a new candidate to discover with capacity to spare *or* an already-queued RESEARCHED entry to re-check, rather than only the former.

- **A node's bare `suitability.mode` is not "is this issue schedulable," because `assess_suitability()` never looks at task state or the local-assessment record**: `/api/dependencies/graph`'s per-node `suitability` payload (added for issue #1227) was documented as the classifier's "authoritative" verdict, but `assess_suitability()` has no access to `task.state` (state exclusion lives entirely in `DependencyGraph.get_ready_tasks()`'s own `_EXCLUDED_FROM_READY` filter, applied *after* classification) or to a project's `session_factory` (only `queue_maintenance` resolves the local `task_assessments` fallback; building the graph has no DB access). A `DONE` or `IN_PROGRESS` node carrying `agent:ready` therefore still classifies `mode="autonomous"`, and a consumer that read the bare mode as "spawnable" would act on finished or in-flight issues. `to_dict()` now also computes `suitability.eligible = mode in AGENT_ELIGIBLE_MODES and task.state not in _EXCLUDED_FROM_READY` per node, matching `get_ready_tasks()`'s own combined filter, and the docstring now says explicitly that `mode`/`reasons`/`blocked_by` reflect labels and dependency state alone: a consumer deciding whether an issue is schedulable must read `suitability.eligible`, never the bare `mode`. The local-assessment gap is left undocumented-but-named rather than closed, since closing it would mean threading a `session_factory` into graph construction purely for display purposes.

- **The dependency-gate's open-dependency walk is now the same function the classifier calls, not a parallel reimplementation**: `check_dependency_gate()` (`sova/supervisor/gates/dependency.py`) predates `assess_suitability()` and kept its own line-for-line copy of "walk declared dependencies, skip epics, treat a missing or non-DONE dependency as blocking" even after the classifier's consolidation, the one readiness decision issue #1227 left re-deriving that half. `sova/supervisor/suitability.py:unsatisfied_dependencies(issue: int, graph)` is now the public, task-id-taking version of that walk (the private `_unsatisfied_dependencies(task, graph)` wrapper just resolves `task.id` to an int, handling the Jira-id case, and delegates); `check_dependency_gate()` calls it and formats only the first unsatisfied dependency into its `BlockReason` (the walk sorts dependency ids, so which one surfaces first is now deterministic rather than set-iteration-order-dependent, a behavior change no test depended on).

- **A typo'd log level must never crash the caller, and an epic's block stays deliberately unscoped by pipeline candidate**: `log_once()` (`sova/utils/log_dedup.py`) used to resolve the emit method with a bare `getattr(log, level)`, so a typo'd or non-stdlib `level` argument (`"warn"`, `"critcal"`) raised `AttributeError` out of what is meant to be a safe logging helper, and the dedup entry was recorded (by then already past the point of no return in the old ordering) before the crash, making the failure non-reproducible on retry. `level` is now checked against a small allowlist (`debug`/`info`/`warning`/`error`/`critical`); an unrecognised value logs an `invalid_level` warning and falls back to `warning` instead of raising, and the dedup entry is recorded only once the emit target is resolved. Separately, `check_human_involvement_gate()`'s `type: epic` label block (added alongside the classifier consolidation) is deliberately unscoped by `candidate`, exactly like the pre-existing `agent:human-only` block: an issue that acquires the label after a PR already exists stays blocked for every later pipeline stage, including address-review and integrate, until the label is removed. This is a visible design choice, not an oversight (`test_epic_label_blocks_post_development_candidate_too` pins it); the local-*assessment*-record fallback is the one piece of this gate that is scoped to pre-development candidates, since unlike a label it never self-clears. `check_human_involvement_gate()`'s `issue` parameter is typed `int | str` to match this: it accepts a Jira-style string id as well as a GitHub int, since `Task.id` itself is a `str` and this gate never builds a `DependencyGraph` (which is the only thing that would reject a non-numeric id).

- **A git-diff-level guard must run in the PR's own worktree on the PR's own branch ref, diff the base from the pre-run fork point, and confirm blob content before flagging a revert; a dependency pin diff line with no name capture must be paired by its enclosing lockfile key, not index, name, or raw adjacency; a deleted file is not a confirmed revert; and a downgrade check must diff what this run introduced, not the PR's whole history**: `sova/core/pr_diff_guard.py` is the pure, LLM-free git-diff counterpart of `invariants/silent-revert-guard.sh`, wired into `_validate_address_pr()`'s and `_validate_rearrange_commits()`'s outcome validation (`sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`, both via the shared `_check_pr_diff_guard()`) for dashboard-spawned `command:address-pr` and `command:rearrange-commits` runs: these are the two commands whose job is to rebase and squash a branch's history after it may already have landed on GitHub, and `_check_pr_diff_guard()` generalizes its own messages to the agent's actual command name (`_strip_command_prefix(agent.role)`) rather than hardcoding "address-pr" for both. The autonomous address-review pipeline's own `RearrangeCommitsStep` (role `developer`, not a `command:` role) is not covered by this validator and remains unguarded: wiring it in needs a baseline SHA captured once at the start of the pipeline (before `RebaseStep` and `AddressReviewStep` run, not just before the rearrange step itself) threaded through `ExecutionContext`, which was deliberately left out of this round's fix as a separate, larger change. A narrower gap in the `command:rearrange-commits` path itself was fixed instead: `start_command()` only ever captured `pre_run_sha` from a PR's `headRefOid`, so an operator invocation with no PR number never had a baseline and the validator entry for it could never actually fire. `AgentState.pre_run_head_sha` now captures the resolved worktree's local `git rev-parse HEAD` at spawn time, only when there is no `pr_number` (`_capture_worktree_head_sha()`), and `_check_pr_diff_guard()` falls back to it (`agent.pre_run_sha if ... is not None else agent.pre_run_head_sha`) so the validator has a usable baseline either way. `_check_pr_diff_guard()` runs against `agent.worktree_path`, preferring it over `agent.project_dir` only as a fallback for a run that has no worktree: a PR-based command run never executes in the primary checkout (`agent_lifecycle.py::start_command` aborts first if `cwd.resolve() == project_dir.resolve()`), so resolving `HEAD` against `project_dir` would inspect a different checkout entirely (this repo keeps its primary checkout on `main`), making the guard either silently inert or comparing the wrong trees; `AgentState.worktree_path` is set from the already-resolved `cwd` in both `start_agent()` and `start_command()`. The guard also resolves the PR's actual branch name via `gh pr view --json headRefName` (the same lookup `_check_pr_branch_pushed()` already makes) and diffs against that ref explicitly rather than trailing `HEAD`, so a worktree left mid-rebase with a detached `HEAD` is still diffed correctly; a `network_blocked` branch-name lookup surfaces as `OutcomeValidation.network_blocked` rather than a bare failure, same as every other GitHub-dependent tier. The same lookup also fetches the PR's own `baseRefName` (`[.headRefName, .baseRefName] | @tsv`) and uses it as the diff's base branch instead of always trailing `cfg.base_branch`: for a stacked PR (based on another feature branch) or a release/maintenance branch, diffing against the project-wide default would attribute everything the real base contributed to "the base changed it since the fork", manufacturing or masking candidates; `cfg.base_branch` is kept only as the fallback when there is no PR number or the lookup is network-blocked. `check_silent_revert()` first checks that `pre_run_sha` (often the PR branch's remote head captured before the command started) actually resolves locally via `git cat-file -e <sha>^{commit}`, retrying after a `git fetch origin <head_ref>` if not: a commit pushed from elsewhere (the GitHub web UI, another machine) between that capture and this worktree's last fetch of the branch may not exist yet, which is "cannot verify", not "verified bad", so a still-missing baseline returns `GuardResult(ok=True)` rather than failing closed. `_check_pr_diff_guard()` treats that skip as final rather than falling back to the baseline SHA for the pin-downgrade diff (an earlier round's bug): when `GuardResult.pre_merge_base`/`post_merge_base` come back `None`, the whole guard returns valid immediately, since re-diffing pin downgrades against the same SHA `check_silent_revert()` just proved unresolvable would only rediscover the identical failure as a false `network_blocked` outcome. It then computes the PR's own file set both before (`pre_run_sha`) and after (the resolved branch ref) the run, each against its own fork point, and the base's own change set from the *pre-run* fork point, not the post-run one: address-pr's normal job is to rebase onto the current base, which makes the post-run fork point equal to the base tip and that diff empty, hiding exactly the "forked before a sibling fix landed, rebase silently dropped it" scenario the guard exists to catch; the sibling's change lives between the pre-run fork point and the current base tip. A file that is both new to the diff and part of that base change set is only a *candidate*, not a confirmed revert: the branch may have made its own new edit to a file the base also happened to touch, which looks identical at the path-set level. `_confirm_content_reverted()` resolves each candidate's blob SHA at the branch head, the pre-run fork point, and the base ref (`git rev-parse <ref>:<path>`) and returns a `(reverted, inconclusive)` pair rather than a single fail-closed bool: the head blob being unresolvable (the branch deleted the path) while the pre-run blob resolved (the path existed before this run) is a deliberate deletion, not "the old content survived", and is reported `inconclusive=True`; the pre-run blob being unresolvable (the path never existed at the fork point at all, e.g. the base added it after the fork) means there is no pre-sibling content for the branch to have reverted, so a branch head that edits the base's own new file (typically after rebasing onto it) is `(False, False)`, not a confirmed revert, which an earlier round had backward. `check_silent_revert()`'s candidate loop evaluates every candidate rather than returning on the first inconclusive one, and a confirmed revert always wins over an inconclusive deletion: a run that both legitimately deleted one file and silently reverted another reports the revert and names the file that matters, turning into a "branch deleted `<path>`" `GuardResult.inconclusive` only when no candidate in the same run was a confirmed revert; only an unresolvable base blob stays fail-closed as a confirmed revert. It fetches `origin/<base_branch>` itself before computing any merge-base (bumped to a 60s timeout from an original 15s, which was tight enough for a large repo or a slow link to misreport a real push as inconclusive): the worktree's local ref could otherwise be stale from whatever it last fetched, silently missing a sibling PR that merged to the base in the window since. `GuardResult.inconclusive` is the field that keeps this fetch, and every other "could not resolve" branch (an unresolvable merge-base, a failed `git diff`), from being treated as a confirmed revert: `_check_pr_diff_guard()` reads it back into `OutcomeValidation.network_blocked`, which `agent_finalize.py` maps to a `status="interrupted"` TaskRun rather than `"failed"`, so an outage or a local git hiccup cannot consume the `pipeline.max_address_review_cycles` budget or report a false revert; only an actual confirmed-content revert, or an actual confirmed pin downgrade, produces `inconclusive=False`'s hard failure. `check_pin_downgrades()` is the second, independent check the same validator runs, and it runs twice: once from the *pre-run* fork point to `agent.pre_run_sha` (what already existed, and was presumably already reviewed, before this run started) and once from `GuardResult.post_merge_base` to the resolved branch ref (what exists now), with only the `(file, package)` pairs present in the second set and absent from the first reported as "introduced by this run". Diffing only the post-run side from the pre-run SHA (the original design) over-attributed: a PR whose first, already-reviewed commit intentionally downgraded a pin would fail every subsequent address-pr run on that same PR, since the downgrade was always present in that span regardless of what the run itself did. `check_pin_downgrades()` returns `(downgrades, inconclusive)` rather than a bare list: `diff_file_set()` distinguishes "git failed" (`None`) from "nothing changed" (an empty set), and collapsing both into "no downgrades" would silently disable the whole check on a git failure, exactly the "failure must not look like success" pattern AGENTS.md forbids; `_check_pr_diff_guard()` treats either side's `inconclusive=True` the same as a `GuardResult.inconclusive` revert check, via `OutcomeValidation.network_blocked`. The parser drops `package.json`'s own metadata fields (`version`, `engines`, `node`, ...) first since they match the same "quoted key, quoted digit-leading value" shape as a real dependency pin but are not one. A downgrade is exempted only when *both* the downgraded file and the package name are named in the operator's own intent (`_is_pin_downgrade_exempted()`, a case-insensitive substring match, deliberately dependency-free rather than a structured allowlist): matching the file name alone was too permissive, since a file like `requirements.txt` or `pyproject.toml` appears in almost any Python dependency-adjacent spec regardless of which package it actually names; the nameless lockfile fallback (`"<lockfile entry>"`, used only when no enclosing package key could be resolved) is still exempted on the file name alone, since there is no real package name to match. `_resolve_pin_downgrade_exemption_text()` checks the issue's spec document (`sova.core.spec_utils.read_spec()`) and tracker body (`create_adapter(cfg).get_task()`) ahead of `agent.prompt`, since for any project with the command installed under `.claude/commands/` the prompt is just `"/address-pr pr=123"` (`agent_context.py::_resolve_command_prompt`), which can never name a file; each lookup is independently best-effort so a missing spec, an unassigned issue, or an adapter failure never blocks the outcome check. `_parse_pin_lines()` branches on whether its regex has a `name` capture group (`pattern.groupindex`): `_PYTHON_REQUIREMENT_RE` and `_PACKAGE_JSON_DEP_RE` both capture the package name on the same line as its version and so are grouped into a `{name: (removed, added)}` dict, but `_NPM_LOCK_RE` matches a lockfile's bare `"version": "..."` line, which carries no package identifier at all. Pairing those by raw line adjacency alone (the original fix for the "all version lines collapse into one dict key" bug) still mispaired an ordinary dependency replacement: when `npm install` swaps package A for package B, git emits A's entire removed entry immediately before B's entire added entry, so A's "-version" line sits directly before B's "+version" line with nothing in common between them, and adjacency alone reported B's version as a downgrade of A. The fix tags each version line with the most recently seen enclosing lockfile object key (a line matching `"<key>": {`, the shape both npm lockfile formats use to open a package's entry) and pairs a removal with the next addition only when both share that key, discarding a removal whose key never recurs; verified against a real two-commit `git diff` fixture (not a hand-built diff string, which is exactly what hid the bug) that replaces one dependency and genuinely downgrades another in the same diff. A v2/v3 lockfile's root self-entry (`"packages": { "": { "version": ... } }`) sits inside the `"packages"` container but is keyed by an empty string, which never matches the key-open pattern (it requires at least one character), so its version line was still tagged with the surrounding `"packages"` key and reported as a downgrade of a nonexistent package; `_LOCKFILE_CONTAINER_KEYS` now skips a version line whose tracked key is a container (`packages`, `dependencies`, `devDependencies`, `optionalDependencies`, `peerDependencies`, `engines`) rather than an actual per-package entry. Separately, `_parse_pin_lines_named()` (the name-capturing path used by `_PYTHON_REQUIREMENT_RE`/`_PACKAGE_JSON_DEP_RE`) used to key its `(removed, added)` pair on the bare package name for the whole file, so a package pinned in two places (package.json's `dependencies` and `devDependencies`, or pyproject.toml's main and optional-dependency groups) had its second occurrence silently overwrite the first, masking a real downgrade entirely; it now collects each occurrence in diff order and pairs the Nth removal of a name with the Nth addition of that same name. Files: `sova/core/pr_diff_guard.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_db.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_pool.py`, `sova/dashboard/services/agent_lifecycle.py`. Tests: `tests/test_pr_diff_guard.py`, `tests/test_dashboard.py::TestCheckPrDiffGuard`, `tests/test_dashboard.py::TestCommandOutcomeValidation`.

- **A full check's reuse marker must exclude itself from the worktree-state hash it is keyed to, or writing it invalidates it**: `develop.md` Step 6, `simplify.md` Step 3, `review.md` Step 7, and `pr.md` Step 8 each tell the dispatched agent to run the project's full check command, duplicating the same check within one autonomous developer-pipeline cycle when nothing in the worktree changed between those call sites (`SimplifyStep` runs immediately after `DevelopStep`, so its check is the one most often re-run against byte-identical content: a cycle in which no simplification is applied re-ran the whole suite for nothing). `sova.core.check_cache` lets a later call site skip a re-run when an earlier one already proved the identical worktree content passes: `should_skip()`/`record_result()` hash the commit the worktree diff is taken against + the full working-tree diff (staged, unstaged, untracked) + the check command string itself, and persist the outcome to a worktree-local marker (`.claude/agent-control/check-state.json`, following the convention `sova.ipc.handoff` already uses for cross-process state). The hash computation (`_compute_hash()`) had to exclude `.claude/agent-control/` from its own untracked-file listing via a pathspec (`git ls-files --others --exclude-standard -- . ":(exclude).claude/agent-control"`): writing the marker file creates a new untracked path inside the worktree, so without the exclusion, the very act of recording a passing result changes the listing the next hash computation sees, making every marker stale the instant it is written on any project that has not (or not yet) gitignored that directory; this repo's own `.gitignore` already covers it, which is why the self-invalidation only surfaced in a test fixture repo with no `.gitignore` at all, not in production use, and the exclusion was added anyway so correctness never depends on a project's `.gitignore` being set up a particular way. That base commit is `_diff_base()`'s merge base with the base branch (`origin/HEAD`, else `origin/main`/`origin/master`, falling back to `HEAD` when no such ref is reachable, e.g. a fixture repo with no remote), never `HEAD` itself: a diff taken against `HEAD` changes the instant `CommitStep` commits already-checked work (the pending diff empties and `HEAD` moves) even though the files the check command reads are byte-identical, which would discard `review.md`'s recorded pass before `pr.md` on the other side of that commit, the very duplicate the cache exists to remove. `_compute_hash()` returns `None` rather than folding a failed `git` call's empty output into the payload (AGENTS.md, "Failure must not look like success"): a hash built from a partial answer can collide with a different worktree's, so a transient failure (an `index.lock` held by a concurrent `git` in the same worktree) must never let "could not determine the state" read as "the state matches". `record_result()` likewise returns a bool and swallows an unhashable worktree or an unwritable marker path, and `sova checks record` always exits 0: the documented call is chained after the real check, so a non-zero exit from advisory bookkeeping would report a passing check as a failing one and send the agent into its fix-and-re-run loop over nothing. The exit code is not enough on its own, which is why the markdown chains it as `<check> && { sova checks record <check> --passed || true; }`: a project's globally installed `sova` can predate this subcommand entirely (including this repo's own editable install, which resolves to the primary checkout on `main`), and Typer answers an unknown subcommand with exit 2 before any of this module runs, which an unguarded `&&` chain would again surface as a failed check. `should-skip` needs no guard: a non-zero exit there already means "run the check". Reuse is deliberately scoped to autonomous pipeline dispatches via `PIPELINE_STAGE_ENV_KEY` (`SOVA_PIPELINE_STAGE`): `sova.core.agent_dispatch._run_agent_process()` sets it to `"1"` in the env handed to every tool-using step's spawned agent (`runtime.spawn(env={**os.environ, PIPELINE_STAGE_ENV_KEY: "1"}, ...)`), since `dispatch_command()`/`dispatch_prompt()` are only ever called from a `BaseStep.execute()` inside a `WorkflowEngine`-run pipeline; a human's own interactive Claude Code session typing `/develop` directly, and the dashboard's one-off `agent_lifecycle.start_command()` spawn (which calls `runtime.spawn()` directly, bypassing `agent_dispatch.py` entirely), never see the var, so `should_skip()` always returns `False` for them and the command's documented full check always runs for real, satisfying the requirement that a standalone `/develop`, `/review`, or `/pr` invocation stays fully deterministic outside the autonomous chain. `DevelopStep._run_inner_check_loop()` and `ValidateStep`'s pre-push regression check are deliberately untouched: both remain mandatory full checks that always execute, since they are the two authoritative gates (the TDD fix loop and the pre-push gate) the issue requires to survive this change; only the command-level markdown instructions that duplicate work those two already guarantee were made conditional on the cache. `WorktreeStep.execute()` and `CaptureBaselineStep.execute()` both call `clear_marker()` on the (re)claimed worktree path, so a worktree reused across cycles (same path, a different issue or branch) never inherits a marker whose hash happens to coincidentally match the new cycle's content. The standalone marketplace plugin (`plugins/sova/`) ships to projects that never installed SOVA, so `sova/commands/marketplace_export.py` collapses the whole `if sova checks should-skip ...` block back to the plain check command there: rendered verbatim it would be a syntactically plausible bash script invoking a CLI that does not exist, with the plugin's prose `check_cmd` placeholder as its quoted argument, and an agent following the "execute fenced bash blocks literally" contract would run it. `tests/test_marketplace_plugin.py::test_no_hard_sova_dependencies` guards the collapse. Each call site records its pass only from inside that `<check> && { sova checks record ... || true; }` chain, never as a separate follow-up step: an agent in a fix loop usually re-runs just the failing tests or the linter, so a record issued outside the chain would key a full-check pass to a worktree state the full check never ran against, and the next call site would skip its own run on the strength of it. Files: `sova/core/check_cache.py`, `sova/cli/commands/checks.py`, `sova/core/agent_dispatch.py`, `sova/core/steps/create_worktree.py`, `sova/core/steps/capture_baseline.py`, `sova/commands/marketplace_export.py`, `sova/commands/data/commands/develop.md`, `sova/commands/data/commands/simplify.md`, `sova/commands/data/commands/review.md`, `sova/commands/data/commands/pr.md`. Issue #1158.
